[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Lynching_of_Jesse_Washington%2C_1916_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-Lynching_of_Jesse_Washington%2C_1916_%28cropped%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lynching_of_Jesse_Washington,_1916_(cropped).jpg)

[Lynching of Jesse Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_Jesse_Washington "Lynching of Jesse Washington") in [Waco, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waco,_Texas "Waco, Texas"), on May 15, 1916. He was repeatedly lowered and raised onto a fire for about two hours. A professional photographer took pictures of the lynching as it unfolded.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/31/Hanging_in_Georgia_%28black_man_hung_by_a_white_mob%29_%28NBY_7660%29.jpg/250px-Hanging_in_Georgia_%28black_man_hung_by_a_white_mob%29_%28NBY_7660%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Hanging_in_Georgia_(black_man_hung_by_a_white_mob)_(NBY_7660).jpg)

Lynching of John William Clark in [Cartersville, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cartersville,_Georgia "Cartersville, Georgia"), September 1930, after killing Police Chief J. B. Jenkins<sup id="cite_ref-2"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-2"><span>[</span>2<span>]</span></a></sup>

[Lynching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching "Lynching") was the occurrence of [extrajudicial killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killing "Extrajudicial killing") that began in the United States' [pre–Civil War South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antebellum_South "Antebellum South") in the 1830s and had mostly ended by time of the [civil rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_rights_movement "Civil rights movement") in the 1950s and 1960s, although instances occurred as late [as 1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_Michael_Donald "Lynching of Michael Donald"). Although the victims of lynchings were members of various ethnicities, after roughly 4 million [enslaved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States "Slavery in the United States") [African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Americans "African Americans") were emancipated, they became the primary victims of lynchings by [white Southerners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Southerners "White Southerners"). Lynchings in the U.S. reached their height from the 1890s to the 1920s, and they primarily victimized [ethnic minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_minority "Ethnic minority"). Most of the lynchings occurred in the [American South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_United_States "Southern United States"), as the majority of African Americans lived there, but [racially motivated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_in_the_United_States "Racism in the United States") lynchings also occurred in the [Midwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States "Midwestern United States") and the border states of the [Southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_United_States "Southwestern United States"), where [Mexicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexicans "Mexicans") were often the victims of lynchings.<sup id="cite_ref-Eji_3-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Eji-3"><span>[</span>3<span>]</span></a></sup> In 1891, the [largest single mass lynching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1891_New_Orleans_lynchings "1891 New Orleans lynchings") (11) in American history was perpetrated in [New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans "New Orleans") against [Italian immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Americans "Italian Americans").<sup id="cite_ref-4"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-4"><span>[</span>4<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-5"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-5"><span>[</span>5<span>]</span></a></sup>

Lynchings followed African Americans with the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_(African_American) "Great Migration (African American)") (c. 1916–1970) out of the American South, and were often perpetrated to enforce [white supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_supremacy "White supremacy") and intimidate ethnic minorities along with other acts of racial terrorism.<sup id="cite_ref-6"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-6"><span>[</span>6<span>]</span></a></sup> A significant number of lynching victims were accused of [murder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder "Murder") or [attempted murder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_murder "Attempted murder"). [Rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rape "Rape"), attempted rape, or other forms of [sexual assault](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_assault "Sexual assault") were the second most common accusation; these accusations were often used as a pretext for lynching African Americans who were accused of violating [Jim Crow era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws "Jim Crow laws") etiquette or engaged in economic competition with [Whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans "White Americans")<sup>[<i><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (August 2025)">citation needed</span></a></i>]</sup>. One study found that there were "4,467 total victims of lynching from 1883 to 1941. Of these victims, 4,027 were men, 99 were women, and 341 were of unidentified gender (although likely male); 3,265 were Black, 1,082 were white, 71 were Mexican or of Mexican descent, 38 were American Indian, 10 were Chinese, and 1 was Japanese."<sup id="cite_ref-7"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-7"><span>[</span>7<span>]</span></a></sup>

A common perception of lynchings in the U.S. is that they were only [hangings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanging "Hanging"), due to the public visibility of the location, which made it easier for photographers to photograph the victims. Some lynchings were professionally photographed and then the photos were [sold as postcards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_postcard "Lynching postcard"), which became popular souvenirs in parts of the United States. Lynching victims were also killed in a variety of other ways: being shot, burned alive, thrown off a bridge, dragged behind a car, etc. Occasionally, the body parts of the victims were removed and sold as souvenirs. Lynchings were not always fatal; "mock" lynchings, which involved putting a rope around the neck of someone who was suspected of concealing information, was sometimes used to compel people to make "confessions". Lynch mobs varied in size from just a few to thousands.

Lynching steadily increased after the Civil War, peaking in 1892. Lynchings remained common into the early 1900s, accelerating with the emergence of the [Second Ku Klux Klan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Ku_Klux_Klan "Second Ku Klux Klan"). Lynchings declined considerably by the time of the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression "Great Depression"). The 1955 lynching of [Emmett Till](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmett_Till "Emmett Till"), a 14-year-old [African-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American "African-American") boy, galvanized the civil rights movement and marked the last classical lynching (as recorded by the [Tuskegee Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_University "Tuskegee University")). The overwhelming majority of lynching perpetrators never faced justice. White supremacy and all-white juries ensured that perpetrators, even if tried, would not be convicted. Campaigns against lynching gained momentum in the early 20th century, championed by groups such as the [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP "NAACP"). Some 200 anti-lynching bills were introduced in Congress between the end of the Civil War and the Civil Rights movement, but none passed. In 2022, 67 years after Emmett Till's killing and the end of the lynching era, the United States Congress passed anti-lynching legislation in the form of the [Emmett Till Antilynching Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmett_Till_Antilynching_Act "Emmett Till Antilynching Act").

Collective violence was a familiar aspect of the early American legal landscape, with group violence in colonial America being usually nonlethal in intention and result. In the 17th century, in the context of the [Wars of the Three Kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_the_Three_Kingdoms "Wars of the Three Kingdoms") in the British Isles and unsettled social and political conditions in the American colonies, lynchings became a frequent form of "mob justice" when the authorities were perceived as untrustworthy.<sup id="cite_ref-Pfeifer,_Michael_J._2011_8-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Pfeifer,_Michael_J._2011-8"><span>[</span>8<span>]</span></a></sup> In the United States, during the decades after the [Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War "American Civil War"), African Americans were the main victims of racial lynching, but in the [American Southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_United_States "Southwestern United States"), [Mexican Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Americans "Mexican Americans") were also the targets of lynching as well.<sup id="cite_ref-9"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-9"><span>[</span>9<span>]</span></a></sup>

At the first recorded lynching, in [St. Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis,_Missouri "St. Louis, Missouri") in 1835, a Black man named McIntosh (who killed a deputy sheriff while being taken to jail) was captured, chained to a tree, and burned to death on a corner lot downtown in front of a crowd of more than 1,000 people.<sup id="cite_ref-10"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-10"><span>[</span>10<span>]</span></a></sup>

According to historian Michael J. Pfeifer, the prevalence of lynchings in post–Civil War America reflected people's lack of confidence in the "[due process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Due_process "Due process")" of the U.S. judicial system. He links the decline in lynchings in the early 20th century to "the advent of the modern death penalty", and argues that "legislators renovated the death penalty...out of direct concern for the alternative of mob violence". Between 1901 and 1964, Georgia hanged and electrocuted 609 people. Eighty-two percent of those executed were Black men, even though Georgia was majority white. Pfeifer also cited "the modern, racialized excesses of urban police forces in the twentieth century and after" as bearing characteristics of lynchings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPfeifer20047–9_11-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPfeifer20047%E2%80%939-11"><span>[</span>11<span>]</span></a></sup>

## Lynching as a means to maintain white supremacy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=2 "Edit section: Lynching as a means to maintain white supremacy")\]

### Lines of continuity from slavery to present

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=3 "Edit section: Lines of continuity from slavery to present")\]

A major motive for lynchings, particularly in the South, was white society's efforts to maintain [white supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_supremacy "White supremacy") after the [emancipation of enslaved people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirteenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution "Thirteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution") following the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War "American Civil War"). Lynchings punished perceived violations of customs, later institutionalized as [Jim Crow laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws "Jim Crow laws"), which mandated racial segregation of Whites and Blacks, and second-class status for Blacks. A 2017 paper found that more racially segregated counties were more likely to be places where Whites conducted lynchings.<sup id="cite_ref-12"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-12"><span>[</span>12<span>]</span></a></sup>

Lynchings emphasized the new social order which was constructed under Jim Crow; Whites acted together, reinforcing their collective identity along with the unequal status of Blacks through these group acts of violence.<sup id="cite_ref-13"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-13"><span>[</span>13<span>]</span></a></sup>

Lynchings were also (in part) intended as a [voter suppression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_suppression_in_the_United_States "Voter suppression in the United States") tool. Lynchings occurred more frequently in proximity to elections, in particular in areas where the Democratic Party faced challenges.<sup id="cite_ref-14"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-14"><span>[</span>14<span>]</span></a></sup> Lynchings were also more likely in the aftermath of elections where the Democratic Party performed poorly.<sup id="cite_ref-15"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-15"><span>[</span>15<span>]</span></a></sup>

### Tuskegee definition

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=4 "Edit section: Tuskegee definition")\]

Tuskegee Institute, now [Tuskegee University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_University "Tuskegee University"), defined conditions that constituted a recognized lynching, a definition which became generally accepted by other compilers of the era:

> 1\. There must be legal evidence that a person was killed.  
> 2\. That person must have met death illegally.  
> 3\. A group of three or more persons must have participated in the killing.  
> 4\. The group must have acted under the pretext of service to justice, race, or tradition.<sup id="cite_ref-16"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-16"><span>[</span>16<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Bibliography_of_data_sources_17-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Bibliography_of_data_sources-17"><span>[</span>17<span>]</span></a></sup>

Statistics for lynchings have traditionally come from three sources primarily, none of which covered the entire historical time period of lynching in the United States. Before 1882, no contemporaneous statistics were assembled on a national level. In 1882, the _[Chicago Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Tribune "Chicago Tribune")_ began to systematically tabulate lynchings nationally. In 1908, the [Tuskegee Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_Institute "Tuskegee Institute") began a systematic collection of lynching reports under the direction of [Monroe Work](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monroe_Work "Monroe Work") at its Department of Records, drawn primarily from newspaper reports. Monroe Work published his first independent tabulations in 1910, although his report also went back to the starting year 1882.<sup id="cite_ref-18"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-18"><span>[</span>18<span>]</span></a></sup> Finally, in 1912, the [National Association for the Advancement of Colored People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_for_the_Advancement_of_Colored_People "National Association for the Advancement of Colored People") started an independent record of lynchings. The numbers of lynchings from each source vary slightly, with the Tuskegee Institute's figures being considered "conservative" by some historians.<sup id="cite_ref-gibson_19-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-gibson-19"><span>[</span>19<span>]</span></a></sup>

Based on the source, the numbers vary depending on which sources are cited, the years that are considered by those sources, and the definitions that are given to specific incidents by those sources. The [Tuskegee Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_University "Tuskegee University") has recorded the lynchings of 3,446 Blacks and the lynchings of 1,297 Whites, all of which occurred between 1882 and 1968, with the peak occurring in the 1890s, at a time of economic stress in the South and increasing political suppression of Blacks.<sup id="cite_ref-tuskegee_umkc_20-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-tuskegee_umkc-20"><span>[</span>20<span>]</span></a></sup> A six-year study published in 2017 by the [Equal Justice Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Justice_Initiative "Equal Justice Initiative") found that 4,084 Black men, women, and children fell victim to "racial terror lynchings" in twelve Southern states between 1877 and 1950, besides 300 that took place in other states. During this period, Mississippi's 654 lynchings led the lynchings which occurred in all of the Southern states.<sup id="cite_ref-21"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-21"><span>[</span>21<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Eji_3-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Eji-3"><span>[</span>3<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-22"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-22"><span>[</span>22<span>]</span></a></sup>

The records of Tuskegee Institute remain the single most complete source of statistics and records on this crime since 1882 for all states, although modern research has illuminated new incidents in studies focused on specific states in isolation.<sup id="cite_ref-Bibliography_of_data_sources_17-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Bibliography_of_data_sources-17"><span>[</span>17<span>]</span></a></sup> As of 1959, which was the last time that Tuskegee Institute's annual report was published, a total of 4,733 persons had died by lynching since 1882. The last lynching recorded by the Tuskegee Institute was that of Emmett Till in 1955. In the 65 years leading up to 1947, at least one lynching was reported every year. The period from 1882 to 1901 saw the height of lynchings, with an average of more than 150 each year. 1892 saw the most number of lynchings in a year: 231 or 3.25 per one million people. After 1924 cases steadily declined, with less than 30 a year.<sup id="cite_ref-1926..._23-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-1926...-23"><span>[</span>23<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Tuskegeefigures_24-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Tuskegeefigures-24"><span>[</span>24<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Thurston_33-36_25-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Thurston_33-36-25"><span>[</span>25<span>]</span></a></sup> The decreasing rate of yearly lynchings was faster outside the South and for white victims of lynching. Lynching became more of a Southern phenomenon and a racial one that overwhelmingly affected Black victims.<sup id="cite_ref-New_York_1944_page_561_26-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-New_York_1944_page_561-26"><span>[</span>26<span>]</span></a></sup> There were measurable variations in lynching rates between and within states.<sup id="cite_ref-27"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-27"><span>[</span>27<span>]</span></a></sup>

Table 3.8: Black victims of lynchings per 100,000 blacks, by state, 1882-1930 (from _A festival of violence : an analysis of Southern lynchings, 1882-1930_ by Tolnay & Beck, 1995, pg. 38)
| State | No. of victims per 100,000 |
| --- | --- |
| Mississippi | 52.8 |
| Georgia | 41.8 |
| Louisiana | 43.7 |
| Alabama | 32.4 |
| South Carolina | 18.8 |
| Florida | 79.8 |
| Tennessee | 38.4 |
| Arkansas | 42.6 |
| Kentucky | 45.7 |
| North Carolina | 11.0 |

Cause of Lynchings per Tuskegee Institute<sup id="cite_ref-New_York_1944_page_561_26-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-New_York_1944_page_561-26"><span>[</span>26<span>]</span></a></sup>
| Cause of Lynching | Percentage of victims |
| --- | --- |
| Murder | 38% |
| Rape | 16% |
| Attempted rape | 7% |
| Felonious assault | 6% |
| Theft | 7% |
| Insult to white person | 2% |
| Miscellaneous/no offence | 24% |

Being lynched for an offence did not mean the victim was guilty of the alleged offence.<sup id="cite_ref-28"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-28"><span>[</span>28<span>]</span></a></sup> In 1940, sociologist [Arthur F. Raper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_F._Raper "Arthur F. Raper") investigated one hundred lynchings after 1929 and estimated that approximately one-third of the victims were falsely accused.<sup id="cite_ref-New_York_1944_page_561_26-2"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-New_York_1944_page_561-26"><span>[</span>26<span>]</span></a></sup>

For the miscellaneous causes, Raper (1968) reported that for lynchings between 1889 and 1929, these included: inciting racial unrest, frightening school children, bringing a lawsuit against white men, kidnapping, using offensive language, engaging in a fight between white and black men, stealing farm animals, attempting to gain employment in a restaurant, killing farm animals, witchcraft allegations, displaying sympathy for the murder of white men, trying to behave like a white man, running a gambling establishment, allegedly making disrespectful remarks about [Woodrow Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson "Woodrow Wilson"), helping an accused black person escape, criticizing a sailor for participating in a race riot, alleged larceny and expressing sympathy for a black person who had been lynched.<sup id="cite_ref-29"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-29"><span>[</span>29<span>]</span></a></sup>

Tuskegee Institute's method of categorizing most lynching victims as either Black or white in publications and data summaries meant that the murders of some minority and immigrant groups were obscured. In the West, for instance, Mexican, Native Americans, and Chinese were more frequent targets of lynchings than were African Americans, but their deaths were included among those of other Whites. Similarly, although Italian immigrants were the focus of violence in Louisiana when they started arriving in greater numbers, their deaths were not tabulated separately from Whites. In earlier years, Whites who were subject to lynching were often targeted because of suspected political activities or support of freedmen, but they were generally considered members of the community in a way new immigrants were not.<sup id="cite_ref-Carrigan&amp;Webb_30-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Carrigan&amp;Webb-30"><span>[</span>30<span>]</span></a></sup>

Other victims included white immigrants, and, in the Southwest, [Latinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latinos "Latinos"). Of the 468 lynching victims in [Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas "Texas") between 1885 and 1942, 339 were Black, 77 white, 53 Hispanic, and 1 Native American.<sup id="cite_ref-31"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-31"><span>[</span>31<span>]</span></a></sup>

There were also Black-on-Black lynchings, with 125 recorded between 1882 and 1903, and there were four incidences of Whites being killed by Black mobs. The rate of Black-on-Black lynchings rose and fell in similar pattern of overall lynchings. There were also more than 200 cases of white-on-white lynchings in the South before 1930.<sup id="cite_ref-Thurston_33-36_25-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Thurston_33-36-25"><span>[</span>25<span>]</span></a></sup>

Conclusions of numerous studies since the mid-20th century have found the following variables affecting the rate of lynchings in the South: "lynchings were more numerous where the African-American population was relatively large, the agricultural economy was based predominantly on cotton, the white population was economically stressed, the Democratic Party was stronger, and multiple religious organizations competed for congregants."<sup id="cite_ref-32"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-32"><span>[</span>32<span>]</span></a></sup>

### Reconstruction (1865–1877)

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=6 "Edit section: Reconstruction (1865–1877)")\]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Nast%2C_Thomas_%281867-03-23%29._Southern_Justice_._Harper%27s_Weekly_combined.jpg/250px-Nast%2C_Thomas_%281867-03-23%29._Southern_Justice_._Harper%27s_Weekly_combined.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nast,_Thomas_(1867-03-23)._Southern_Justice_._Harper%27s_Weekly_combined.jpg)

This 1867 [Thomas Nast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Nast "Thomas Nast") cartoon called _[Southern Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Justice_(political_cartoon) "Southern Justice (political cartoon)")_ advocates for military intervention to prevent white terrorism in the South

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Kkk-carpetbagger-cartoon.jpg/250px-Kkk-carpetbagger-cartoon.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kkk-carpetbagger-cartoon.jpg)

An 1869 cartoon published in _The Independent Monitor_ of [Tuscaloosa, Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuscaloosa,_Alabama "Tuscaloosa, Alabama"), threatening the lynching of [carpetbaggers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpetbagger "Carpetbagger") by the [Ku Klux Klan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku_Klux_Klan "Ku Klux Klan")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Mississippi_ku_klux.jpg/250px-Mississippi_ku_klux.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mississippi_ku_klux.jpg)

_Mississippi Ku-Klux in the Disguises in Which They Were Captured_, 1872. They were arrested in [Tishomingo County, Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tishomingo_County,_Mississippi "Tishomingo County, Mississippi"), for attempted murder. (Wood engraving from photograph, _Harper's Weekly_, January 27, 1872, Prints and Photographs Division, Library of Congress)<sup id="cite_ref-33"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-33"><span>[</span>33<span>]</span></a></sup>

After the [American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War "American Civil War"), southern Whites struggled to maintain their dominance. Secret vigilante and terrorist groups such as the [Ku Klux Klan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku_Klux_Klan "Ku Klux Klan") (KKK) instigated extrajudicial assaults and killings in order to discourage [freedmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedman "Freedman") from voting, working, and getting educated.<sup id="cite_ref-gibson_19-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-gibson-19"><span>[</span>19<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-New_South_1993_34-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-New_South_1993-34"><span>[</span>34<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Barry_A._Crouch_1868_35-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Barry_A._Crouch_1868-35"><span>[</span>35<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Foner1988p119-123_36-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Foner1988p119-123-36"><span>[</span>36<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-J.C.A._Stagg_1871_37-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-J.C.A._Stagg_1871-37"><span>[</span>37<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_38-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-ReferenceA-38"><span>[</span>38<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Tharoor_39-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Tharoor-39"><span>[</span>39<span>]</span></a></sup> They also sometimes attacked Northerners, teachers, and agents of the [Freedmen's Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedmen%27s_Bureau "Freedmen's Bureau"). The magnitude of the extralegal violence which occurred during election campaigns reached epidemic proportions, leading the historian William Gillette to label it "[guerrilla warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guerrilla_warfare "Guerrilla warfare")",<sup id="cite_ref-New_South_1993_34-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-New_South_1993-34"><span>[</span>34<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Barry_A._Crouch_1868_35-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Barry_A._Crouch_1868-35"><span>[</span>35<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Foner1988p119-123_36-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Foner1988p119-123-36"><span>[</span>36<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-J.C.A._Stagg_1871_37-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-J.C.A._Stagg_1871-37"><span>[</span>37<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_38-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-ReferenceA-38"><span>[</span>38<span>]</span></a></sup> and historian Thomas E. Smith to understand it as a form of "[colonial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonialism "Colonialism") violence".<sup id="cite_ref-40"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-40"><span>[</span>40<span>]</span></a></sup>

The lynchers sometimes murdered their victims, but sometimes whipped or physically assaulted them to remind them of their former status as slaves.<sup id="cite_ref-whipping_41-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-whipping-41"><span>[</span>41<span>]</span></a></sup> Often night-time raids of African American homes were made in order to confiscate firearms. Lynchings to prevent freedmen and their allies from voting and bearing arms were extralegal ways of trying to enforce the previous system of social dominance and the [Black Codes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Codes_(United_States) "Black Codes (United States)"), which had been invalidated by the 14th and 15th Amendments in 1868 and 1870.

Journalist, educator, and civil rights leader [Ida B. Wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ida_B._Wells "Ida B. Wells") commented in an 1892 pamphlet she published called _Southern Horrors: Lynch Law in All Its Phases_ that: "The only times an Afro-American who was assaulted got away has been when he had a gun and used it in self-defense."<sup id="cite_ref-Southern_Horrors:_Lynch_Law_in_All_Its_Phases_by_Ida_B._Wells_(1892)_42-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Southern_Horrors:_Lynch_Law_in_All_Its_Phases_by_Ida_B._Wells_(1892)-42"><span>[</span>42<span>]</span></a></sup> A study published in August 2022 by researchers from [Clemson University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clemson_University "Clemson University") has stated that, "...rates of Black lynching decreased with greater Black firearm access..."<sup id="cite_ref-Reason,_September_8,_2022_43-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Reason,_September_8,_2022-43"><span>[</span>43<span>]</span></a></sup>

From the mid-1870s onward, violence rose as insurgent [paramilitary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramilitary "Paramilitary") groups in the Deep South worked to suppress Black voting and turn Republicans out of office. In Louisiana, the Carolinas, and Florida especially, the Democratic Party relied on paramilitary "White Line" groups, such as the [White Camelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_of_the_White_Camelia "Knights of the White Camelia"), [White League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_League "White League") and [Red Shirts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Shirts_(United_States) "Red Shirts (United States)") to terrorize, intimidate and assassinate African-American and white Republicans in an organized drive to regain power. In Mississippi and the Carolinas, paramilitary chapters of Red Shirts conducted overt violence and disruption of elections. In Louisiana, the [White League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_League "White League") had numerous chapters; they carried out goals of the Democratic Party to suppress Black voting. Grant's desire to keep [Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio "Ohio") in the Republican aisle and his attorney general's maneuvers led to a failure to support the Mississippi governor with Federal troops.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELemann2006135–154_44-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTELemann2006135%E2%80%93154-44"><span>[</span>44<span>]</span></a></sup> The campaign of terror worked. In [Yazoo County, Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yazoo_County,_Mississippi "Yazoo County, Mississippi"), for instance, with an African-American population of 12,000, only seven votes were cast for Republicans in 1874. In 1875, Democrats swept into power in the Mississippi state legislature.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELemann2006135–154_44-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTELemann2006135%E2%80%93154-44"><span>[</span>44<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Henry-smith-2-1-1893-paris-tx-2.jpg/250px-Henry-smith-2-1-1893-paris-tx-2.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Henry-smith-2-1-1893-paris-tx-2.jpg)

The 1893 public lynching of black teenager [Henry Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Smith_(lynching_victim) "Henry Smith (lynching victim)") in [Paris, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris,_Texas "Paris, Texas")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/Lynching_of_Laura_Nelson%2C_May_1911.jpg/250px-Lynching_of_Laura_Nelson%2C_May_1911.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lynching_of_Laura_Nelson,_May_1911.jpg)

The lynching of [Laura Nelson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_Laura_and_L._D._Nelson "Lynching of Laura and L. D. Nelson") in [Okemah, Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okemah,_Oklahoma "Okemah, Oklahoma"), on May 25, 1911<sup id="cite_ref-45"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-45"><span>[</span>45<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-46"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-46"><span>[</span>46<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/af/Lynching_of_Bennie_Simmons.jpg/250px-Lynching_of_Bennie_Simmons.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lynching_of_Bennie_Simmons.jpg)

Lynching of Bennie Simmons, soaked in coal oil before being set on fire. June 13, 1913, Oklahoma

Lynching attacks on African Americans, especially in the [South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_United_States "Southern United States"), increased dramatically in the aftermath of Reconstruction. The peak of lynchings occurred in 1892, after White [Southern Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Democrats "Southern Democrats") had regained control of state legislatures. Many incidents were related to economic troubles and competition. At the turn of the 20th century, southern states passed new constitutions or legislation which effectively [disenfranchised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disenfranchisement_after_the_Reconstruction_era "Disenfranchisement after the Reconstruction era") most Black people and many [Poor Whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poor_White "Poor White"), established [segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation "Racial segregation") of public facilities by race, and separated Black people from common public life and facilities through [Jim Crow laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws "Jim Crow laws"). The rate of lynchings in the South has been strongly associated with economic strains,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeckTolnay1990_47-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeckTolnay1990-47"><span>[</span>47<span>]</span></a></sup> although the causal nature of this link is unclear.<sup id="cite_ref-48"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-48"><span>[</span>48<span>]</span></a></sup> Low cotton prices, [inflation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation "Inflation"), and economic stress are associated with higher frequencies of lynching.

[Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_(U.S._state) "Georgia (U.S. state)") led the nation in lynchings from 1900 to 1931, with 302 incidents, according to The [Tuskegee Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_University "Tuskegee University"). However, [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida "Florida") led the nation in lynchings [per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Per_capita "Per capita") from 1900 to 1930.<sup id="cite_ref-49"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-49"><span>[</span>49<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-50"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-50"><span>[</span>50<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-51"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-51"><span>[</span>51<span>]</span></a></sup> Lynchings peaked in many areas when it was time for landowners to settle accounts with [sharecroppers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharecropper "Sharecropper").<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154–155_52-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154%E2%80%93155-52"><span>[</span>52<span>]</span></a></sup>

The frequency of lynchings rose during years of poor economy and low prices for cotton, demonstrating that more than social tensions generated the catalysts for mob action against the underclass.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBeckTolnay1990_47-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeckTolnay1990-47"><span>[</span>47<span>]</span></a></sup> Researchers have studied various models to determine what motivated lynchings. One study of lynching rates of Blacks in Southern counties between 1889 and 1931 found a relation to the concentration of Blacks in parts of the Deep South: where the Black population was concentrated, lynching rates were higher. Such areas also had a particular mix of socioeconomic conditions, with a high dependence on cotton cultivation.<sup id="cite_ref-53"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-53"><span>[</span>53<span>]</span></a></sup>

[Henry Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Smith_(lynching_victim) "Henry Smith (lynching victim)"), an African-American handyman accused of murdering a policeman's daughter, was a noted lynching victim because of the ferocity of the attack against him and the huge crowd that gathered.<sup id="cite_ref-matters_54-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-matters-54"><span>[</span>54<span>]</span></a></sup> He was lynched at [Paris, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris,_Texas "Paris, Texas"), in 1893 for killing Myrtle Vance, the three-year-old daughter of a Texas policeman, after the policeman had assaulted Smith.<sup id="cite_ref-55"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-55"><span>[</span>55<span>]</span></a></sup> Smith was not tried in a court of law. A large crowd followed the lynching, as was common then in the style of public executions. Henry Smith was fastened to a wooden platform, tortured for 50 minutes by red-hot iron brands, and burned alive while more than 10,000 spectators cheered.<sup id="cite_ref-matters_54-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-matters-54"><span>[</span>54<span>]</span></a></sup>

Fewer than one percent of lynch mob participants were ever convicted by local courts and they were seldom prosecuted or brought to trial. By the late 19th century, trial juries in most of the southern United States were all white because African Americans had been disenfranchised, and only registered voters could serve as jurors. Often juries never let the matter go past the inquest.

Such cases happened in the North as well. In 1892, a police officer in [Port Jervis, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_Jervis,_New_York "Port Jervis, New York"), tried to stop the lynching of a Black man who had been wrongfully accused of assaulting a white woman. The mob responded by putting the noose around the officer's neck as a way of scaring him, and completed killing the other man. Although at the inquest the officer identified eight people who had participated in the lynching, including the former chief of police, the jury determined that the murder had been carried out "by person or persons unknown".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPfeifer200435_56-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPfeifer200435-56"><span>[</span>56<span>]</span></a></sup>

In [Duluth, Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duluth,_Minnesota "Duluth, Minnesota"), on June 15, 1920, [three young African-American traveling circus workers were lynched](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duluth_lynchings "Duluth lynchings") after having been accused of having raped a white woman and were jailed pending a grand jury hearing. A physician's subsequent examination of the woman found no evidence of rape or assault.<sup>[<i><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="The text near this tag needs further explanation. (October 2018)">further explanation needed</span></a></i>]</sup><sup id="cite_ref-57"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-57"><span>[</span>57<span>]</span></a></sup>

In 1903, the _[St. Louis Post-Dispatch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis_Post-Dispatch "St. Louis Post-Dispatch")_ reported a new, popular make-believe children's game: "The Game of Lynching". "Imaginary mayor gives order not to harm imaginary mob, and an imaginary hanging follows. Fire contributes realistic touch." "It has crowded out baseball", and if it continues, "may deprive of some of its prestige the game of football".<sup id="cite_ref-58"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-58"><span>[</span>58<span>]</span></a></sup>

### The new Ku Klux Klan

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=8 "Edit section: The new Ku Klux Klan")\]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c1/Birth-of-a-nation-klan-and-black-man.jpg/250px-Birth-of-a-nation-klan-and-black-man.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Birth-of-a-nation-klan-and-black-man.jpg)

A scene from the 1915 movie, _[The Birth of a Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Birth_of_a_Nation "The Birth of a Nation")_, showing an African-American character, Gus (played by white actor [Walter Long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Long_(actor) "Walter Long (actor)"), in [blackface](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackface "Blackface")), about to be killed by the Ku Klux Klan

[D. W. Griffith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D._W._Griffith "D. W. Griffith")'s 1915 film, _[The Birth of a Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Birth_of_a_Nation "The Birth of a Nation")_, glorified the original [Ku Klux Klan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku_Klux_Klan "Ku Klux Klan") as protecting white southern women during Reconstruction, which he portrayed as a time of [violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence "Violence") and [corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption "Corruption"), following the [Dunning School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunning_School "Dunning School")'s interpretation of history. The film aroused great controversy. It was popular among Whites nationwide, but it was protested against by Black activists, the [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP "NAACP") and other [civil rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_and_political_rights "Civil and political rights") groups.<sup id="cite_ref-59"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-59"><span>[</span>59<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-60"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-60"><span>[</span>60<span>]</span></a></sup>

On November 25, 1915, a group of men led by [William J. Simmons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Joseph_Simmons "William Joseph Simmons") burned a cross on top of [Stone Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_Mountain "Stone Mountain"), inaugurating the revival of the Ku Klux Klan. The event was attended by 15 charter members and a few aging former members of the original Klan.<sup id="cite_ref-time_61-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-time-61"><span>[</span>61<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Film_still_of_lynching_from_Micheaux%27s_Within_Our_Gates.jpg/250px-Film_still_of_lynching_from_Micheaux%27s_Within_Our_Gates.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Film_still_of_lynching_from_Micheaux%27s_Within_Our_Gates.jpg)

A scene from the 1920 movie, _[Within Our Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Within_Our_Gates "Within Our Gates")_, showing the lynching of film characters, Jasper Landry and his wife

The Klan and their use of lynching was supported by some public officials like [John Trotwood Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Trotwood_Moore "John Trotwood Moore"), the [State Librarian and Archivist of Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_State_Library_and_Archives "Tennessee State Library and Archives") from 1919 to 1929.<sup id="cite_ref-patriciancult_62-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-patriciancult-62"><span>[</span>62<span>]</span></a></sup> Moore "became one of the South's more strident advocates of lynching".<sup id="cite_ref-patriciancult_62-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-patriciancult-62"><span>[</span>62<span>]</span></a></sup>

One of the worst years for lynching was 1919, with at least 76 people killed in mob- or vigilante-related violence. Of these, more than 11 African-American veterans who had served in the recently completed war were [lynched in that year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_veterans_lynched_after_World_War_I "African-American veterans lynched after World War I").<sup id="cite_ref-Williams_63-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Williams-63"><span>[</span>63<span>]</span></a></sup><sup><span title="Page / location: 232">: 232 </span></sup>  White supremacist violence culminated in the 1921 [Tulsa race massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa_race_massacre "Tulsa race massacre") in which the majority-Black [Greenwood District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwood_District,_Tulsa "Greenwood District, Tulsa") was razed to the ground.

The NAACP mounted a strong nationwide campaign of protests and public education against _The Birth of a Nation_. As a result, some city governments prohibited the release of the film. In addition, the NAACP publicized production and helped create audiences for the 1919 releases, _[The Birth of a Race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Birth_of_a_Race "The Birth of a Race")_ and _[Within Our Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Within_Our_Gates "Within Our Gates")_, African-American–directed films that presented more positive images of blacks.

### During and after the Great Depression

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=9 "Edit section: During and after the Great Depression")\]

While the frequency of lynching dropped in the 1930s, there was a spike in 1930 during the Great Depression. For example, in North Texas and southern Oklahoma alone, four people were lynched in separate incidents in less than a month. A spike in lynchings occurred after [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II"), as tensions arose after veterans returned home. White people tried to re-impose white supremacy over returning Black veterans. [The last documented mass lynching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moore%27s_Ford_lynchings "Moore's Ford lynchings") occurred in [Walton County, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walton_County,_Georgia "Walton County, Georgia") in 1946, when local white landowners killed two war veterans and their wives.

Soviet media frequently covered racial discrimination in the U.S.<sup id="cite_ref-64"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-64"><span>[</span>64<span>]</span></a></sup> Deeming American criticism of Soviet Union human rights abuses at this time as hypocrisy, the Russians responded with "[And you are lynching Negroes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/And_you_are_lynching_Negroes "And you are lynching Negroes")".<sup id="cite_ref-65"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-65"><span>[</span>65<span>]</span></a></sup> In _Cold War Civil Rights: Race and the Image of American Democracy_ (2001), the historian [Mary L. Dudziak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_L._Dudziak "Mary L. Dudziak") wrote that Soviet communist criticism of racial discrimination and violence in the United States influenced the federal government to support civil rights legislation.<sup id="cite_ref-66"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-66"><span>[</span>66<span>]</span></a></sup>

Most lynchings ceased by the 1960s.<sup id="cite_ref-tuskegee_umkc_20-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-tuskegee_umkc-20"><span>[</span>20<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-tuskegee_umkc2_67-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-tuskegee_umkc2-67"><span>[</span>67<span>]</span></a></sup> However, in 2021, there were claims that racist lynchings still happen in the United States, being covered up as suicides.<sup id="cite_ref-68"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-68"><span>[</span>68<span>]</span></a></sup>

### Justifications of lynchings

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=10 "Edit section: Justifications of lynchings")\]

Opponents of legislation often said lynchings prevented murder and rape. As documented by [Ida B. Wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ida_B._Wells "Ida B. Wells"), the most prevalent accusation against lynching victims was murder or attempted murder. Rape charges or rumors were present in less than one-third of the lynchings; such charges were often pretexts for lynching Blacks who violated [Jim Crow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws "Jim Crow laws") etiquette or engaged in economic competition with Whites. Other common reasons given included arson, theft, assault, and robbery; sexual transgressions ([miscegenation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miscegenation "Miscegenation"), adultery, cohabitation); "race prejudice", "race hatred", "racial disturbance"; informing on others; "threats against whites"; and violations of the color line ("attending white girl", "proposals to white woman").<sup id="cite_ref-SouthernHorrors_69-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-SouthernHorrors-69"><span>[</span>69<span>]</span></a></sup>

Although the rhetoric which surrounded lynchings frequently suggested that they were carried out in order to protect the virtue and safety of white women, the actions basically arose out of white attempts to maintain domination in a rapidly changing society and their fears of social change.<sup id="cite_ref-gibson_19-2"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-gibson-19"><span>[</span>19<span>]</span></a></sup>

Mobs usually alleged crimes for which they lynched Black people. In the late 19th century, however, journalist Ida B. Wells showed that many presumed crimes were either exaggerated or had not even occurred.<sup id="cite_ref-70"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-70"><span>[</span>70<span>]</span></a></sup>

The [ideology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideology "Ideology") behind lynching, directly connected to the denial of political and social equality, was stated forthrightly in 1900 by [United States Senator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate "United States Senate") [Benjamin Tillman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Tillman "Benjamin Tillman"), who was previously [governor of South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_South_Carolina "Governor of South Carolina"):

> We of the South have never recognized the right of the negro to govern white men, and we never will. We have never believed him to be the equal of the white man, and we will not submit to his gratifying his lust on our wives and daughters without lynching him.<sup id="cite_ref-herbert_71-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-herbert-71"><span>[</span>71<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-72"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-72"><span>[</span>72<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Lynching-of-will-james.jpg/250px-Lynching-of-will-james.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lynching-of-will-james.jpg)

The lynching of African American [William "Froggie" James](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_%22Froggie%22_James "William "Froggie" James") in [Cairo, Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo,_Illinois "Cairo, Illinois"), on November 11, 1909. A crowd of thousands watched the lynching.<sup id="cite_ref-73"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-73"><span>[</span>73<span>]</span></a></sup>

Lynchings declined briefly after White supremacists, the so-called "[Redeemers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redeemers "Redeemers")", took over the governments of the southern states in the 1870s. By the end of the 19th century, with struggles over labor and disenfranchisement, and continuing agricultural depression, lynchings rose again. Between 1882 and 1968, the [Tuskegee Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_Institute "Tuskegee Institute") recorded 1,297 lynchings of white people and 3,446 lynchings of Black people.<sup id="cite_ref-74"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-74"><span>[</span>74<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-75"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-75"><span>[</span>75<span>]</span></a></sup> Lynchings were concentrated in the [Cotton Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_Belt_(region) "Cotton Belt (region)") ([Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi "Mississippi"), [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_(U.S._state) "Georgia (U.S. state)"), [Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama "Alabama"), [Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas "Texas"), and [Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana "Louisiana")).<sup id="cite_ref-76"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-76"><span>[</span>76<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-77"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-77"><span>[</span>77<span>]</span></a></sup> However, lynchings of [Mexicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexicans "Mexicans") were under-counted in the Tuskegee Institute's records,<sup id="cite_ref-78"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-78"><span>[</span>78<span>]</span></a></sup> and some of the largest mass lynchings in American history were the [Chinese massacre of 1871](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_massacre_of_1871 "Chinese massacre of 1871") and the [lynching of eleven Italian immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1891_New_Orleans_lynchings "1891 New Orleans lynchings") in 1891 in [New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans "New Orleans").<sup id="cite_ref-79"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-79"><span>[</span>79<span>]</span></a></sup> During the [California Gold Rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Gold_Rush "California Gold Rush"), White resentment towards more successful Mexican and Chilean miners resulted in the lynching of at least 160 Mexicans between 1848 and 1860.<sup id="cite_ref-80"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-80"><span>[</span>80<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-81"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-81"><span>[</span>81<span>]</span></a></sup>

Members of mobs that participated in lynchings often took photographs of what they had done to their victims in order to spread awareness and fear of their power. Souvenir taking, such as pieces of rope, clothing, branches, and sometimes [body parts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_trophy_collecting "Human trophy collecting") was not uncommon. Some of those photographs were published and sold as postcards. In 2000, [James Allen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Allen_(collector) "James Allen (collector)") published a collection of 145 lynching photos in book form as well as online,<sup id="cite_ref-82"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-82"><span>[</span>82<span>]</span></a></sup> with written words and video to accompany the images.

The murders reflected the tensions of labor and social changes, as the Whites imposed [Jim Crow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow "Jim Crow") rules, legal [segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation "Racial segregation") and white supremacy. The lynchings were also an indicator of long economic stress due to falling cotton prices through much of the 19th century, as well as financial depression in the 1890s. In the Mississippi bottomlands, for instance, lynchings rose when crops and accounts were supposed to be settled.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154–155_52-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154%E2%80%93155-52"><span>[</span>52<span>]</span></a></sup>

### In the Mississippi Delta region

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=11 "Edit section: In the Mississippi Delta region")\]

The late 1800s and early 1900s in the Mississippi Delta showed both frontier influence and actions directed at repressing African Americans. After the Civil War, 90% of the Delta was still undeveloped.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154–155_52-2"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154%E2%80%93155-52"><span>[</span>52<span>]</span></a></sup> Both Whites and Blacks migrated there for a chance to buy land in the backcountry. It was frontier wilderness, heavily forested and without roads for years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154–155_52-3"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154%E2%80%93155-52"><span>[</span>52<span>]</span></a></sup> Before the start of the 20th century, lynchings often took the form of frontier justice directed at transient workers as well as residents.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154–155_52-4"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154%E2%80%93155-52"><span>[</span>52<span>]</span></a></sup> Thousands of workers were brought in by planters to do lumbering and work on levees.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWillis2000[httpswwwgooglecombookseditionForgotten_Times1hfSMdOLrkChlengbpv1dqmississippideltathousandsworkerslevees19thcenturypgPA157printsecfrontcover_157]_83-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillis2000[httpswwwgooglecombookseditionForgotten_Times1hfSMdOLrkChlengbpv1dqmississippideltathousandsworkerslevees19thcenturypgPA157printsecfrontcover_157]-83"><span>[</span>83<span>]</span></a></sup>

Whites accounted for just over 12 percent of the Delta region's population, but made up nearly 17 percent of lynching victims. So, in this region, they were lynched at a rate that was more than 35 percent higher than their proportion in the population, primarily due to being accused of crimes against property (chiefly theft). Conversely, Blacks were lynched at a rate, in the Delta, lesser than their proportion of the population. This was unlike the rest of the South, where Blacks comprised the majority of lynching victims. In the Delta, they were most often accused of murder or attempted murder, in half the cases, and 15 percent of the time, they were accused of rape, meaning that another 15 percent of the time they were accused of a combination of rape and murder, or rape and attempted murder.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154–155_52-5"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154%E2%80%93155-52"><span>[</span>52<span>]</span></a></sup>

A clear seasonal pattern to lynchings existed with colder months being the deadliest. As noted, cotton prices fell during the 1880s and 1890s, increasing economic pressures. "From September through December, the cotton was picked, debts were revealed, and profits (or losses) realized... Whether concluding old contracts or discussing new arrangements, \[landlords and tenants\] frequently came into conflict in these months and sometimes fell to blows."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154–155_52-6"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillis2000154%E2%80%93155-52"><span>[</span>52<span>]</span></a></sup> During the winter, murder was most cited as a cause for lynching. After 1901, as economics shifted and more Blacks became renters and sharecroppers in the Delta, with few exceptions, only African Americans were lynched. The frequency increased from 1901 to 1908 after African Americans were disfranchised. "In the twentieth century Delta vigilantism finally became predictably joined to white supremacy."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWillis2000157_84-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWillis2000157-84"><span>[</span>84<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Jim_miller_hanging.jpg/250px-Jim_miller_hanging.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jim_miller_hanging.jpg)

From left to right, the lynching of killer-for-hire [Jim Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Miller_(outlaw) "Jim Miller (outlaw)") and three others in [Ada, Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ada,_Oklahoma "Ada, Oklahoma"), on April 19, 1909

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Lynching_of_Leo_Frank_1.jpg/250px-Lynching_of_Leo_Frank_1.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lynching_of_Leo_Frank_1.jpg)

The lynching of [Leo Frank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Frank "Leo Frank") in [Marietta, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marietta,_Georgia "Marietta, Georgia"), on August 17, 1915. Judge Morris, who organized the crowd after the lynching, is on the far right and he is wearing a straw hat.

According to the [Tuskegee Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuskegee_University "Tuskegee University"), of the 4,743 people lynched between 1882 and 1968, 1,297 were listed as "white". The Tuskegee Institute, which kept the most complete records, documented victims internally as "Negro", "white", "Chinese", and occasionally as "Mexican" or "Indian", but merged these into only two categories of Black or white in the tallies it published. Mexican, Chinese, and Native American lynching victims were tallied as white. Particularly in the West, minorities such as Chinese, [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Americans_in_the_United_States "Native Americans in the United States"), Mexicans, and others were also lynching victims. The lynching of Mexicans and [Mexican Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Americans "Mexican Americans") in the Southwest was long overlooked in American history, when attention was focused on the treatment of African Americans in the South.<sup id="cite_ref-Tuskegeefigures_24-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Tuskegeefigures-24"><span>[</span>24<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Carrigan&amp;Webb_30-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Carrigan&amp;Webb-30"><span>[</span>30<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-85"><span>[</span>85<span>]</span></a></sup>

In modern scholarship, researchers estimate that 597 Mexicans were lynched between 1848 and 1928. Mexicans were lynched at a rate of 27.4 per 100,000 of population between 1880 and 1930. This statistic was second only to that of the African-American community, which endured an average of 37.1 per 100,000 of population during that period. Between 1848 and 1879, Mexicans were lynched at an unprecedented rate of 473 per 100,000 of population.<sup id="cite_ref-Carrigan&amp;Webb_30-2"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Carrigan&amp;Webb-30"><span>[</span>30<span>]</span></a></sup>

After their increased immigration to the U.S. in the late 19th century, [Italian Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Americans "Italian Americans") in the South were recruited for laboring jobs. On March 14, 1891, [11 Italian immigrants were lynched](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1891_New_Orleans_lynchings "1891 New Orleans lynchings") in [New Orleans, Louisiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans,_Louisiana "New Orleans, Louisiana").<sup id="cite_ref-86"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-86"><span>[</span>86<span>]</span></a></sup> This incident was one of the largest mass lynchings in U.S. history.<sup id="cite_ref-87"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-87"><span>[</span>87<span>]</span></a></sup> A total of 20 Italians were lynched during the 1890s. Although most lynchings of Italian Americans occurred in the South, Italians did not comprise a major portion of immigrants or a major portion of the population as a whole. Isolated lynchings of Italians also occurred in [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_(state) "New York (state)"), [Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania "Pennsylvania"), and [Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado "Colorado").

On February 21, 1909, [a riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Town_riot "Greek Town riot") targeting Greek Americans occurred in [Omaha, Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omaha,_Nebraska "Omaha, Nebraska"). Many Greeks were looted, beaten, and businesses were burnt.

In 1915, [Leo Frank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Frank "Leo Frank"), an [American Jew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Jews "American Jews"), was lynched near [Atlanta, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta,_Georgia "Atlanta, Georgia"). In 1913, Frank had been convicted of the murder of Mary Phagan, a 13-year-old girl who was employed by his pencil factory. A series of appeals were filed on behalf of Frank, but all of them were denied. The final appeal was denied after a 7–2 decision was made by the [U.S. Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States "Supreme Court of the United States"). After Governor [John M. Slaton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_M._Slaton "John M. Slaton") commuted Frank's sentence to [life imprisonment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_imprisonment "Life imprisonment"), a group of men, calling themselves the Knights of Mary Phagan, kidnapped Frank from a prison farm in [Milledgeville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milledgeville,_Georgia "Milledgeville, Georgia") in a planned event that included cutting the prison's telephone wires. They transported him 175 miles back to [Marietta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marietta,_Georgia "Marietta, Georgia"), near Atlanta, where they lynched him in front of a mob.

After the lynching of Leo Frank, around half of Georgia's 3,000 Jews left the state.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETheoharisCox198845_88-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTETheoharisCox198845-88"><span>[</span>88<span>]</span></a></sup> According to author Steve Oney, "What it did to [Southern Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_in_the_Southern_United_States "Jews in the Southern United States") can't be discounted... It drove them into a state of denial about their Judaism. They became even more [assimilated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_assimilation "Jewish assimilation"), [anti-Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Zionism "Anti-Zionism"), [Episcopalian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Church_(United_States) "Episcopal Church (United States)"). The Temple did away with [chupahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chupah "Chupah") at weddings – anything that would draw attention."<sup id="cite_ref-89"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-89"><span>[</span>89<span>]</span></a></sup>

Between the 1830s and 1850s, the majority of those lynched were Whites. More Whites were lynched than Blacks for the years 1882–1885. By 1890s, the number of Blacks lynched yearly grew to a number significantly more than that of Whites, and the vast majority of victims were Black from then on. White people were mostly lynched in the Western states and territories, although there were more than 200 cases in the South. According to the Tuskegee Institute, in 1884 near [Georgetown, Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown,_Colorado "Georgetown, Colorado"), there was one instance of 17 "unknown white men" being hanged as cattle thieves in a single day. In the West, lynchings were often done to establish law and order.<sup id="cite_ref-gibson_19-3"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-gibson-19"><span>[</span>19<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-tuskegee_umkc_20-2"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-tuskegee_umkc-20"><span>[</span>20<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Thurston_33-36_25-2"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Thurston_33-36-25"><span>[</span>25<span>]</span></a></sup>

### Spectacle lynching and racist memorabilia

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=13 "Edit section: Spectacle lynching and racist memorabilia")\]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/Anon_lynching%2C_c_1900%2C_unknown_location%2C_US.jpg/250px-Anon_lynching%2C_c_1900%2C_unknown_location%2C_US.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Anon_lynching,_c_1900,_unknown_location,_US.jpg)

Body of a lynched Black male, propped up in a rocking chair for a photograph, c. 1900. Paint has been applied to his face, circular disks glued to his cheeks, cotton glued to his face and head, while a rod props up the victim's head.

Lynching was often treated as a spectator sport, where large crowds gathered to partake in or watch the torture and mutilation of the victim(s). The attendants often treated these as festive events, with food, family photos, and souvenirs.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_90-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-:0-90"><span>[</span>90<span>]</span></a></sup> Whites used these events to demonstrate their power and control. This often led to the exodus of the remaining Black population and discouraged future settlement.<sup id="cite_ref-Eji_3-2"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Eji-3"><span>[</span>3<span>]</span></a></sup>

In the post–Reconstruction era, lynching photographs were printed for various purposes, including postcards, newspapers, and event mementos.<sup id="cite_ref-Ifill,_Sherrilyn_A_2007_91-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Ifill,_Sherrilyn_A_2007-91"><span>[</span>91<span>]</span></a></sup> Typically these images depicted an African-American lynching victim and all or part of the crowd in attendance. Spectators often included women and children. The perpetrators of lynchings were not identified.<sup id="cite_ref-Goff,_Jennie_2011_92-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Goff,_Jennie_2011-92"><span>[</span>92<span>]</span></a></sup> The 1916 lynching of [Jesse Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_Jesse_Washington "Lynching of Jesse Washington") in Waco, Texas, drew nearly 15,000 spectators.<sup id="cite_ref-Ifill,_Sherrilyn_A_2007_91-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Ifill,_Sherrilyn_A_2007-91"><span>[</span>91<span>]</span></a></sup> Often lynchings were advertised in newspapers prior to the event in order to give photographers time to arrive early and prepare their camera equipment.<sup id="cite_ref-Kim,_Linda_2014_93-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Kim,_Linda_2014-93"><span>[</span>93<span>]</span></a></sup>

#### Photographic records and postcards

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=14 "Edit section: Photographic records and postcards")\]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2e/Postcard_of_the_lynched_Will_Stanley_front_and_back.jpg/250px-Postcard_of_the_lynched_Will_Stanley_front_and_back.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Postcard_of_the_lynched_Will_Stanley_front_and_back.jpg)

The front and back of a postcard showing the charred corpse of Will Stanley in [Temple, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temple,_Texas "Temple, Texas"), in 1915. Joe Meyers marked the back of the postcard to show his parents he was in the crowd: "This is the Barbecue we had last night.. my picture is to the left with a cross over it.. your son Joe".<sup id="cite_ref-:0_90-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-:0-90"><span>[</span>90<span>]</span></a></sup>

At the start of the 20th century in the United States, lynching was a photographic sport. People sent picture postcards of lynchings they had witnessed. A writer for _Time_ magazine noted in 2000,

> Even the [Nazis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism "Nazism") did not stoop to selling souvenirs of [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz "Auschwitz"), but lynching scenes became a burgeoning subdepartment of the postcard industry. By 1908, the trade had grown so large, and the practice of sending postcards featuring the victims of mob murderers had become so repugnant, that the [U.S. Postmaster General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Postmaster_General "United States Postmaster General") banned the cards from the mails.<sup id="cite_ref-Lacayo_94-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Lacayo-94"><span>[</span>94<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Duluth-lynching-postcard.jpg/250px-Duluth-lynching-postcard.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Duluth-lynching-postcard.jpg)

Postcard of the [1920 Duluth, Minnesota lynchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1920_Duluth_lynchings "1920 Duluth lynchings"). Two of the Black victims are still hanging while the third is on the ground. Postcards of lynchings were popular souvenirs in the U.S.<sup id="cite_ref-Moyers_95-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Moyers-95"><span>[</span>95<span>]</span></a></sup>

After the lynching, photographers would sell their pictures as-is or as postcards, sometimes costing as much as 50 cents a piece, or $9, as of 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-Goff,_Jennie_2011_92-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Goff,_Jennie_2011-92"><span>[</span>92<span>]</span></a></sup> Though some photographs were sold as plain prints, others contained captions. These captions were either straightforward details—such as the time, date and reasons for the lynching—while others contained polemics or poems with racist or otherwise threatening remarks.<sup id="cite_ref-Kim,_Linda_2014_93-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Kim,_Linda_2014-93"><span>[</span>93<span>]</span></a></sup> An example of this is a photographic postcard attached to the poem "Dogwood Tree", which says: "The negro now/By eternal grace/Must learn to stay in the negro's place/In the Sunny South, the land of the Free/Let the WHITE SUPREME forever be."<sup id="cite_ref-96"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-96"><span>[</span>96<span>]</span></a></sup> Such postcards, with explicit rhetoric such as "Dogwood Tree", were typically circulated privately or mailed in a sealed envelope.<sup id="cite_ref-Apel,_Dora_2004_97-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Apel,_Dora_2004-97"><span>[</span>97<span>]</span></a></sup> Other times, these pictures simply included the word "WARNING".<sup id="cite_ref-Kim,_Linda_2014_93-2"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Kim,_Linda_2014-93"><span>[</span>93<span>]</span></a></sup>
