In 1873, the [Comstock Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comstock_laws "Comstock laws") was passed, which banned the publication of "obscene matter as well as its circulation in the mails".<sup id="cite_ref-Kim,_Linda_2014_93-3"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Kim,_Linda_2014-93"><span>[</span>93<span>]</span></a></sup> In 1908, Section 3893 was added to the Comstock Act, stating that the ban included material "tending to incite arson, murder, or assassination".<sup id="cite_ref-Kim,_Linda_2014_93-4"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Kim,_Linda_2014-93"><span>[</span>93<span>]</span></a></sup> Although this act did not explicitly ban lynching photographs or postcards, it banned the explicit racist texts and poems inscribed on certain prints. According to some, these texts were deemed "more incriminating" and caused their removal from the mail instead of the photograph itself because the text made "too explicit what was always implicit in lynchings".<sup id="cite_ref-Kim,_Linda_2014_93-5"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Kim,_Linda_2014-93"><span>[</span>93<span>]</span></a></sup> Some towns imposed "self-censorship" on lynching photographs, but section 3893 was the first step towards a national censorship.<sup id="cite_ref-Kim,_Linda_2014_93-6"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Kim,_Linda_2014-93"><span>[</span>93<span>]</span></a></sup> Despite the amendment, the distribution of lynching photographs and postcards continued. Though they were not sold openly, the censorship was bypassed when people sent the material in envelopes or mail wrappers.<sup id="cite_ref-Apel,_Dora_2004_97-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Apel,_Dora_2004-97"><span>[</span>97<span>]</span></a></sup>

### African American resistance

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=16 "Edit section: African American resistance")\]

African Americans resisted lynchings in numerous ways. Intellectuals and journalists encouraged public education, actively protesting and lobbying against lynch mob violence and government complicity. The [National Association for the Advancement of Colored People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_for_the_Advancement_of_Colored_People "National Association for the Advancement of Colored People") (NAACP), and related groups, organized support from white and Black Americans, publicizing injustices, investigating incidents, and working for passage of federal [anti-lynching legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dyer_Anti-Lynching_Bill "Dyer Anti-Lynching Bill") (which finally passed as the [Emmett Till Anti-Lynching Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmett_Till_Antilynching_Act "Emmett Till Antilynching Act") on March 29, 2022).<sup id="cite_ref-:1_98-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-:1-98"><span>[</span>98<span>]</span></a></sup> African-American women's clubs raised funds and conducted petition drives, letter campaigns, meetings, and demonstrations to highlight the issues and combat lynching.<sup id="cite_ref-Angela_Y._Davis_1983,_pp.194-195_99-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Angela_Y._Davis_1983,_pp.194-195-99"><span>[</span>99<span>]</span></a></sup> In the [great migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_(African_American) "Great Migration (African American)"), particularly from 1910 to 1940, 1.5 million African Americans left the South, primarily for destinations in northern and mid-western cities, both to gain better jobs and education and to escape the high rate of violence. From 1910 to 1930 particularly, more Blacks migrated from counties with high numbers of lynchings.<sup id="cite_ref-tolnay92_100-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-tolnay92-100"><span>[</span>100<span>]</span></a></sup>

African-American writers used their talents in numerous ways to publicize and protest against lynching. In 1914, [Angelina Weld Grimké](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angelina_Weld_Grimk%C3%A9 "Angelina Weld Grimké") had already written her play _[Rachel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_(play) "Rachel (play)")_ to address racial violence. It was produced in 1916. In 1915, [W. E. B. Du Bois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._E._B._Du_Bois "W. E. B. Du Bois"), noted scholar and head of the recently formed NAACP, called for more Black-authored plays.

African American female playwrights were strong in responding. They wrote ten of the 14 anti-lynching plays produced between 1916 and 1935. The NAACP set up a Drama Committee to encourage such work. In addition, [Howard University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_University "Howard University"), the leading historically Black college, established a theater department in 1920 to encourage African-American dramatists. Starting in 1924, the NAACP's major publications _[The Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crisis "The Crisis")_ and _Opportunity_ sponsored contests to encourage Black literary production.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCaskillGebhard2006210–212_101-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCaskillGebhard2006210%E2%80%93212-101"><span>[</span>101<span>]</span></a></sup>

African Americans emerged from the Civil War with the political experience and stature to resist attacks, but disfranchisement and imposition of Jim Crow in the South at the turn of the 20th century closed them out of the political system and judicial system in many ways. Advocacy organizations compiled statistics and publicized the atrocities, as well as working for enforcement of civil rights and a federal anti-lynching law. From the early 1880s, the _[Chicago Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Tribune "Chicago Tribune")_ reprinted accounts of lynchings from other newspapers, and published annual statistics. These provided the main source for the compilations by the Tuskegee Institute to document lynchings, a practice it continued until 1968.<sup id="cite_ref-wexler1_102-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-wexler1-102"><span>[</span>102<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Mary_Garrity_-_Ida_B._Wells-Barnett_-_Google_Art_Project_-_restoration_crop.jpg/250px-Mary_Garrity_-_Ida_B._Wells-Barnett_-_Google_Art_Project_-_restoration_crop.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mary_Garrity_-_Ida_B._Wells-Barnett_-_Google_Art_Project_-_restoration_crop.jpg)

Ida B. Wells exposed lynching in the early 1890s to an international audience

In 1892, journalist [Ida B. Wells-Barnett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ida_B._Wells "Ida B. Wells") was shocked when three friends in [Memphis, Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis,_Tennessee "Memphis, Tennessee"), [were lynched](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People%27s_Grocery_lynchings "People's Grocery lynchings"). She learned it was because their grocery store had competed successfully against a white-owned store. Outraged, Wells-Barnett began a global anti-lynching campaign that raised awareness of these murders. She also investigated lynchings and overturned the common idea that they were based on Black sexual crimes, as was popularly discussed; she found lynchings were more an effort to suppress Blacks who competed economically with Whites, especially if they were successful. As a result of her efforts at education, [Black women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_women "Black women") in the U.S. became active in the anti-lynching crusade, often in the form of clubs that raised money to publicize the abuses. When the [National Association for the Advancement of Colored People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_for_the_Advancement_of_Colored_People "National Association for the Advancement of Colored People") (NAACP) was formed in 1909, Wells became part of its multi-racial leadership and continued to be active against lynching. The NAACP began to publish lynching statistics at their office in New York City.

In 1898, [Alexander Manly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Manly "Alexander Manly") of [Wilmington, North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilmington,_North_Carolina "Wilmington, North Carolina"), directly challenged popular ideas about lynching in an editorial in his newspaper _The Daily Record_. He noted that consensual relationships took place between white women and Black men, and said that many of the latter had white fathers (as he did). His references to [miscegenation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miscegenation "Miscegenation") lifted the veil of denial. Whites were outraged. A mob destroyed his printing press and business, ran Black leaders out of town and killed many others, and [overturned the biracial Populist-Republican city government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilmington_insurrection_of_1898 "Wilmington insurrection of 1898"), headed by a white mayor and majority-white council. Manly escaped, eventually settling in [Philadelphia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia "Philadelphia"), [Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania "Pennsylvania").

In 1903, writer [Charles W. Chesnutt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_W._Chesnutt "Charles W. Chesnutt") of Ohio published the article "The Disfranchisement of the Negro", detailing civil rights abuses as Southern states passed laws and constitutions that essentially [disfranchised African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement_after_the_Reconstruction_era "Disfranchisement after the Reconstruction era"), excluding them wholesale from the political system. He publicized the need for change in the South. Numerous writers appealed to the literate public.<sup id="cite_ref-103"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-103"><span>[</span>103<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/74/A_Man_Was_Lynched_Yesterday_flag.svg/250px-A_Man_Was_Lynched_Yesterday_flag.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:A_Man_Was_Lynched_Yesterday_flag.svg)

[NAACP flag against lynching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_man_was_lynched_yesterday_flag "A man was lynched yesterday flag"), displayed in front of their offices from 1920 to 1938 after each lynching<sup id="cite_ref-nyt_104-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-nyt-104"><span>[</span>104<span>]</span></a></sup>

In 1904, [Mary Church Terrell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Church_Terrell "Mary Church Terrell"), the first president of the [National Association of Colored Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_of_Colored_Women "National Association of Colored Women"), published an article in the magazine _[North American Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Review "North American Review")_ to respond to Southerner [Thomas Nelson Page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Nelson_Page "Thomas Nelson Page"). She analyzed and refuted with data his attempted justification of lynching as a response to assaults by Black men on white women. Terrell showed how apologists like Page had tried to rationalize what were violent mob actions that were seldom based on assaults.<sup id="cite_ref-Angela_Y._Davis_1983,_p.193_105-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Angela_Y._Davis_1983,_p.193-105"><span>[</span>105<span>]</span></a></sup> African-American newspapers such as the Chicago Illinois newspaper _The Chicago Whip_<sup id="cite_ref-106"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-106"><span>[</span>106<span>]</span></a></sup> and the NAACP magazine _[The Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Crisis "The Crisis")_ would not just merely report lynchings, they would denounce them as well. Indeed in 1919, the NAACP would publish "Thirty Years of Lynching" and hang a black flag outside its office.<sup id="cite_ref-107"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-107"><span>[</span>107<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/TulsaRaceRiot-1921.png/250px-TulsaRaceRiot-1921.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TulsaRaceRiot-1921.png)

In the 1921 [Tulsa race massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa_race_massacre "Tulsa race massacre") thousands of Whites rampaged through the Black community, killing men and women, burning and looting stores and homes. Up to 300 Blacks were killed

African-American resistance to lynching carried substantial risks. In 1921, in [Tulsa, Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa,_Oklahoma "Tulsa, Oklahoma"), a group of 75 African-American citizens attempted to stop a lynch mob from taking 19-year-old assault suspect [Dick Rowland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Rowland "Dick Rowland") out of jail. In a scuffle between a white man and an armed African-American veteran, the white man was shot, leading to a shootout between the two groups, which left two African Americans and 10 Whites dead. Whites retaliated by rioting, during which they burned 1,256 homes and as many as 200 businesses in the segregated [Greenwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwood,_Tulsa,_Oklahoma "Greenwood, Tulsa, Oklahoma") district, destroying what had been a thriving area. A [2001 state commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa_Reparations_Coalition "Tulsa Reparations Coalition") confirmed 39 dead, 26 Black and 13 white deaths. The commission gave several estimates ranging from 75 to 300 total dead.<sup id="cite_ref-108"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-108"><span>[</span>108<span>]</span></a></sup> Rowland was saved, however, and was later exonerated.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Mary_Burnett_Talbert.jpg/250px-Mary_Burnett_Talbert.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mary_Burnett_Talbert.jpg)

[Mary Burnett Talbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Burnett_Talbert "Mary Burnett Talbert") served as National Director of the NAACP Anti-Lynching Campaign in 1921

The growing networks of African-American women's club groups were instrumental in raising funds to support the NAACP's public education and lobbying campaigns. They also built community organizations. In 1922, [Mary Talbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Talbert "Mary Talbert") headed the anti-lynching crusade to create an integrated women's movement against lynching.<sup id="cite_ref-Angela_Y._Davis_1983,_p.193_105-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Angela_Y._Davis_1983,_p.193-105"><span>[</span>105<span>]</span></a></sup> It was affiliated with the NAACP, which mounted a multi-faceted campaign. For years the NAACP used petition drives, letters to newspapers, articles, posters, lobbying Congress, and marches to protest against the abuses in the South and keep the issue before the public.

A 2022 study found that African-American communities that had increased access to firearms were less likely to be lynched. The study authors write, "In states and years in which Black residents had more access to firearms, there were fewer lynchings... In all three estimation strategy variants, the estimated negative effect of Black firearm access on lynchings is quite large and statistically significant. An increase of one standard deviation in firearm access, for example, is associated with a reduction in lynchings of between 0.8 and 1.4 per year, about half a standard deviation."<sup id="cite_ref-109"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-109"><span>[</span>109<span>]</span></a></sup>

### Resistance by Southern white women

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=17 "Edit section: Resistance by Southern white women")\]

In 1930, Southern white women responded in large numbers to the leadership of [Jessie Daniel Ames](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jessie_Daniel_Ames "Jessie Daniel Ames") in forming the [Association of Southern Women for the Prevention of Lynching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_Southern_Women_for_the_Prevention_of_Lynching "Association of Southern Women for the Prevention of Lynching"). She and her co-founders obtained the signatures of 40,000 women to their pledge against lynching and for a change in the South. The pledge included the statement:

> In light of the facts we dare no longer to... allow those bent upon personal revenge and savagery to commit acts of violence and lawlessness in the name of women.

Despite physical threats and hostile opposition, the women leaders persisted with petition drives, letter campaigns, meetings, and demonstrations to highlight the issues.<sup id="cite_ref-Angela_Y._Davis_1983,_pp.194-195_99-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Angela_Y._Davis_1983,_pp.194-195-99"><span>[</span>99<span>]</span></a></sup> By the 1930s, the number of lynchings had dropped to about ten per year in Southern states.

Cardinal [James Gibbons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Gibbons "James Gibbons") in October 1905 condemned lynching, calling it a blot on American civilization and against the teaching of [Jesus Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_Christ "Jesus Christ").<sup id="cite_ref-110"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-110"><span>[</span>110<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Omaha_courthouse_lynching.jpg/250px-Omaha_courthouse_lynching.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Omaha_courthouse_lynching.jpg)

A group of white men pose for a 1919 photograph as they stand over the Black victim Will Brown who had been lynched and had his body mutilated and burned during the [Omaha race riot of 1919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omaha_race_riot_of_1919 "Omaha race riot of 1919") in [Omaha, Nebraska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omaha,_Nebraska "Omaha, Nebraska")

The rapid influx of Blacks during the [Great Migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Migration_(African_American) "Great Migration (African American)") disturbed the racial balance within Northern cities, exacerbating hostility between Black and white Northerners. The [Red Summer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Summer "Red Summer") of 1919 was marked by hundreds of deaths and higher casualties across the U.S. as a result of race riots that occurred in more than three dozen cities, such as the [Chicago race riot of 1919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_race_riot_of_1919 "Chicago race riot of 1919") and the [Omaha race riot of 1919](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omaha_race_riot_of_1919 "Omaha race riot of 1919").<sup id="cite_ref-111"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-111"><span>[</span>111<span>]</span></a></sup>

## Federal legislation inhibited by the Solid South

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=20 "Edit section: Federal legislation inhibited by the Solid South")\]

At the turn of the century there was a concerted Republican effort to restrict the South's [apportionment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_congressional_apportionment "United States congressional apportionment") of seats<sup id="cite_ref-112"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-112"><span>[</span>112<span>]</span></a></sup> and to set aside cases election results due to [black disenfranchisement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement_after_the_Reconstruction_era "Disfranchisement after the Reconstruction era"). These efforts failed.

President [Theodore Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodore_Roosevelt "Theodore Roosevelt") made public statements against lynching in 1903, following George White's murder in [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware "Delaware"), and in the [1906 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1906_State_of_the_Union_Address "1906 State of the Union Address") on December 4, 1906. When Roosevelt suggested that lynching was taking place in the Philippines, Southern senators (all white Democrats) demonstrated their power by a [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster "Filibuster") in 1902 during review of the "Philippines Bill". In 1903, Roosevelt refrained from commenting on lynching during his Southern political campaigns.

Roosevelt's efforts cost him political support among white people, especially in the South. Threats against him increased so that the [Secret Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Secret_Service "United States Secret Service") added to the size of his bodyguard detail.<sup id="cite_ref-113"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-113"><span>[</span>113<span>]</span></a></sup>

From 1882 to 1968, "nearly 200 anti-lynching bills were introduced in Congress, and three of them passed the House. Seven presidents between 1890 and 1952 petitioned Congress to pass a federal law."<sup id="cite_ref-AP_114-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-AP-114"><span>[</span>114<span>]</span></a></sup> None succeeded in gaining passage, blocked by the [Southern Caucus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Caucus "Southern Caucus") — the delegation of powerful white Southerners in the Senate, which controlled, due to [seniority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seniority_in_the_United_States_Senate "Seniority in the United States Senate"), many of the powerful committee chairmanships.<sup id="cite_ref-AP_114-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-AP-114"><span>[</span>114<span>]</span></a></sup> In 2005, by a resolution sponsored by senators [Mary Landrieu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Landrieu "Mary Landrieu") of Louisiana and [George Allen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Allen_(American_politician) "George Allen (American politician)") of Virginia, and passed by voice vote, the Senate made a formal apology for its failure to pass an anti-lynching law "when it was most needed".<sup id="cite_ref-AP_114-2"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-AP-114"><span>[</span>114<span>]</span></a></sup>

The [Dyer Anti-Lynching Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dyer_Anti-Lynching_Bill "Dyer Anti-Lynching Bill") was first introduced to the [United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Congress "United States Congress") on April 1, 1918, by [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States) "Republican Party (United States)") Congressman [Leonidas C. Dyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonidas_C._Dyer "Leonidas C. Dyer") of [St. Louis, Missouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis,_Missouri "St. Louis, Missouri") in the [U.S. House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._House_of_Representatives "U.S. House of Representatives"). Rep. Dyer was concerned over increased lynching, mob violence, and disregard for the "rule of law" in the South. The bill made lynching a [federal crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_crime_in_the_United_States "Federal crime in the United States"), and those who participated in lynching would be prosecuted by the federal government. It did not pass due to a Southern [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster "Filibuster"). In 1919, the new [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP "NAACP") organized the [National Conference on Lynching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Conference_on_Lynching "National Conference on Lynching") to increase support for the Dyer Bill.

The bill was passed by the [United States House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives "United States House of Representatives") in 1922, and in the same year it was given a favorable report by the [United States Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate "United States Senate") Committee. Its passage was blocked by White Democratic senators from the [Solid South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid_South "Solid South"), the only representatives elected since the southern states [had disfranchised African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement_after_the_Reconstruction_era "Disfranchisement after the Reconstruction era") around the start of the 20th century.<sup id="cite_ref-115"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-115"><span>[</span>115<span>]</span></a></sup> The Dyer Bill influenced later anti-lynching legislation, including the [Costigan-Wagner Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costigan-Wagner_Bill "Costigan-Wagner Bill"), which was also defeated in the US Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-116"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-116"><span>[</span>116<span>]</span></a></sup>

As passed by the House, the Dyer Anti-Lynching Bill stated:

> "To assure to persons within the jurisdiction of every State the equal protection of the laws, and to punish the crime of lynching.... Be it enacted by the Senate and House of Representatives of the United States of America in Congress assembled, That the phrase 'mob or riotous assemblage,' when used in this act, shall mean an assemblage composed of three or more persons acting in concert for the purpose of depriving any person of his life without authority of law as a punishment for or to prevent the commission of some actual or supposed public offense."<sup id="cite_ref-117"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-117"><span>[</span>117<span>]</span></a></sup>

In 1920, the Black community succeeded in getting its most important priority in the Republican Party's platform at the National Convention: support for an anti-lynching bill. The Black community supported [Warren G. Harding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_G._Harding "Warren G. Harding") in that election, but were disappointed as his administration moved slowly on a bill.<sup id="cite_ref-Harvier_118-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Harvier-118"><span>[</span>118<span>]</span></a></sup>

Dyer revised his bill and re-introduced it to the House in 1921. It passed the House, 230 to 119,<sup id="cite_ref-119"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-119"><span>[</span>119<span>]</span></a></sup> on January 26, 1922, due to "insistent country-wide demand",<sup id="cite_ref-Harvier_118-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Harvier-118"><span>[</span>118<span>]</span></a></sup> and was favorably reported out by the [Senate Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Judiciary_Committee "Senate Judiciary Committee"). Action in the Senate was delayed, and ultimately the [Democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States) "Democratic Party (United States)") [Solid South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid_South "Solid South") [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster "Filibuster") defeated the bill in the Senate in December.<sup id="cite_ref-120"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-120"><span>[</span>120<span>]</span></a></sup> In 1923, Dyer went on a Midwestern and Western state tour promoting the anti-lynching bill; he praised the NAACP's work for continuing to publicize lynching in the South and for supporting the federal bill. Dyer's anti-lynching motto was "We have just begun to fight", and he helped generate additional national support. His bill was defeated twice more in the Senate by Southern Democratic filibuster. The Republicans were unable to pass a bill in the 1920s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERuckerUpton2007182–183_121-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTERuckerUpton2007182%E2%80%93183-121"><span>[</span>121<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Lynching_of_two_African_American_men_in_Marion_Indiana_on_7_August_1930_detail%2C_%22Marion%2C_Ind%2C_Aug._7%2C_1930%22_%28NBY_3117%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-Lynching_of_two_African_American_men_in_Marion_Indiana_on_7_August_1930_detail%2C_%22Marion%2C_Ind%2C_Aug._7%2C_1930%22_%28NBY_3117%29_%28cropped%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lynching_of_two_African_American_men_in_Marion_Indiana_on_7_August_1930_detail,_%22Marion,_Ind,_Aug._7,_1930%22_(NBY_3117)_(cropped).jpg)

[Lynching of J. Thomas Shipp and Abraham S. Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_Thomas_Shipp_and_Abram_Smith "Lynching of Thomas Shipp and Abram Smith") in Marion, Indiana, August 7, 1930

### Roosevelt presidency

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=22 "Edit section: Roosevelt presidency")\]

Anti-lynching advocates such as [Mary McLeod Bethune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_McLeod_Bethune "Mary McLeod Bethune") and [Walter Francis White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Francis_White "Walter Francis White") campaigned for presidential candidate [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt "Franklin D. Roosevelt") in 1932. They hoped he would lend public support to their efforts against lynching. In 1934, White prevailed upon Democratic senators [Robert F. Wagner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Wagner "Robert F. Wagner") and [Edward P. Costigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_P._Costigan "Edward P. Costigan") for a new anti-lynching bill which led to the draft of the [Costigan–Wagner Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costigan-Wagner_Bill "Costigan-Wagner Bill") in the same year to require local authorities to protect prisoners from lynch mobs. Like the [Dyer Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dyer_Anti-Lynching_Bill "Dyer Anti-Lynching Bill"), it made lynching a [federal crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_crime_in_the_United_States "Federal crime in the United States") in order to take it out of state administration.<sup id="cite_ref-122"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-122"><span>[</span>122<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/The_Lynching_of_Roosevelt_Townes_and_Robert_McDaniels.jpg/250px-The_Lynching_of_Roosevelt_Townes_and_Robert_McDaniels.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_Lynching_of_Roosevelt_Townes_and_Robert_McDaniels.jpg)

Pictures of the [lynching of Roosevelt Townes and Robert McDaniels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_Roosevelt_Townes_and_Robert_McDaniels "Lynching of Roosevelt Townes and Robert McDaniels") in 1937 were the first photos of lynchings to be published by the national press.

Southerners had a disproportionate influence on Congress. Because of the Southern Democrats' disfranchisement of African Americans in Southern states at the start of the 20th century, Southern Whites for decades had nearly double the representation in Congress beyond their own population. Southern states had Congressional representation based on total population, but essentially only Whites could vote and only their issues were supported. Due to seniority achieved through one-party Democratic rule in their region, Southern Democrats controlled many important committees in both houses. As a result, Southern white Democrats were a formidable power in Congress until the 1960s and they consistently opposed any legislation related to putting lynching under Federal oversight.

In the 1930s, virtually all Southern senators blocked the proposed Costigan–Wagner Bill. Southern senators used a [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_filibuster "Southern filibuster") to prevent a vote on the bill. Some Republican senators, such as the conservative [William Borah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Borah "William Borah") from [Idaho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idaho "Idaho"), opposed the bill for constitutional reasons (he had also opposed the Dyer Bill). He felt it encroached on [state sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution "Tenth Amendment to the United States Constitution") and, by the 1930s, thought that social conditions had changed so that the bill was less needed.<sup id="cite_ref-CR_123-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-CR-123"><span>[</span>123<span>]</span></a></sup> He spoke at length in opposition to the bill in 1935 and 1938. 1934 saw 15 lynchings of African Americans, with 21 lynchings in 1935, eight in 1936, and two in 1939.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Santos_Zingale_Lynch_Law_1934_oil_on_canvas_UWM_Art_Collection_Gift_of_the_Works_Progress_Administration.jpg/250px-Santos_Zingale_Lynch_Law_1934_oil_on_canvas_UWM_Art_Collection_Gift_of_the_Works_Progress_Administration.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Santos_Zingale_Lynch_Law_1934_oil_on_canvas_UWM_Art_Collection_Gift_of_the_Works_Progress_Administration.jpg)

_Lynch Law_ (1934), by [Santos Zingale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santos_Zingale "Santos Zingale") for the [Public Works of Art Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Works_of_Art_Project "Public Works of Art Project")

A lynching in [Fort Lauderdale, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Lauderdale,_Florida "Fort Lauderdale, Florida"), changed the political climate in Washington.<sup id="cite_ref-124"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-124"><span>[</span>124<span>]</span></a></sup> On July 19, 1935, Rubin Stacy, a homeless African-American tenant farmer, knocked on doors begging for food. After resident complaints, deputies took Stacy into custody. While he was in custody, a lynch mob took Stacy from the deputies and murdered him. Although the faces of his murderers could be seen in a photo taken at the lynching site, the state did not prosecute the murder.<sup id="cite_ref-125"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-125"><span>[</span>125<span>]</span></a></sup>

Stacy's murder galvanized anti-lynching activists, but President Roosevelt did not support the federal anti-lynching bill. He feared that support would cost him Southern votes in the [1936 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936_United_States_presidential_election "1936 United States presidential election").

In 1937, the [lynching of Roosevelt Townes and Robert McDaniels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_Roosevelt_Townes_and_Robert_McDaniels "Lynching of Roosevelt Townes and Robert McDaniels") gained national publicity, and its brutality was widely condemned.<sup id="cite_ref-Clarion_126-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Clarion-126"><span>[</span>126<span>]</span></a></sup> Such publicity enabled [Joseph A. Gavagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_A._Gavagan "Joseph A. Gavagan") (D-New York) to gain support for anti-lynching legislation he had put forward in the [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives "United States House of Representatives"); it was supported in the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate "United States Senate") by Democrats [Robert F. Wagner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Wagner "Robert F. Wagner") (New York) and [Frederick Van Nuys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Van_Nuys "Frederick Van Nuys") (Indiana). The legislation eventually passed in the House, but the emerging Democratic [Southern caucus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_caucus "Southern caucus") blocked it in the Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-Finley_127-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Finley-127"><span>[</span>127<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-128"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-128"><span>[</span>128<span>]</span></a></sup> Senator [Allen Ellender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allen_J._Ellender "Allen J. Ellender") (D-Louisiana) proclaimed: "We shall at all cost preserve the white supremacy of America."<sup id="cite_ref-Finley_127-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Finley-127"><span>[</span>127<span>]</span></a></sup>

In 1939, Roosevelt created the [Civil Rights Section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Justice_Civil_Rights_Division "United States Department of Justice Civil Rights Division") of the [Justice Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Justice "United States Department of Justice"). It started prosecutions to combat lynching, but failed to win any convictions until 1946.<sup id="cite_ref-wexler2_129-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-wexler2-129"><span>[</span>129<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Fbi-lynching-poster.jpg/250px-Fbi-lynching-poster.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Fbi-lynching-poster.jpg)

An FBI poster asking the public for information on the 1946 [Moore's Ford lynchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moore%27s_Ford_lynchings "Moore's Ford lynchings") in rural Georgia

In 1946, the Civil Rights Section of the Justice Department gained its first conviction under federal civil rights laws against a lyncher. Florida constable Tom Crews was sentenced to a $1,000 fine (equivalent to $16,100 in 2024) and one year in prison for civil rights violations in the killing of an African-American farm worker.

In 1946, a mob of white men [shot and killed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moore%27s_Ford_lynchings "Moore's Ford lynchings") two young African-American couples near Moore's Ford Bridge in [Walton County, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walton_County,_Georgia "Walton County, Georgia"), 60 miles east of [Atlanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta "Atlanta"). This lynching of four young sharecroppers, one a World War II veteran, shocked the nation. The attack was a key factor in President [Harry S. Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman "Harry S. Truman")'s making civil rights a priority of his administration. Although the [Federal Bureau of Investigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Bureau_of_Investigation "Federal Bureau of Investigation") (FBI) investigated the crime, they were unable to prosecute. It was the last documented lynching of that many individuals in one incident.<sup id="cite_ref-wexler2_129-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-wexler2-129"><span>[</span>129<span>]</span></a></sup>

In 1947, the Truman administration published a report entitled _To Secure These Rights_ which advocated making lynching a federal crime, abolishing poll taxes, and other civil rights reforms. The Southern Democratic bloc of senators and congressmen continued to obstruct attempts at federal legislation.<sup id="cite_ref-130"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-130"><span>[</span>130<span>]</span></a></sup>

In the 1940s, the Klan openly criticized Truman for his efforts to promote civil rights. Later historians documented that Truman had briefly made an attempt to join the Klan as a young man in 1924, when it was near its peak of social influence in promoting itself as a fraternal organization. When a Klan officer demanded that Truman pledge not to hire any [Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church "Catholic Church") if he were re-elected as county judge, Truman refused. He personally knew their worth from his World War I experience. His membership fee was returned.<sup id="cite_ref-131"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-131"><span>[</span>131<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-135"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-135"><span>[</span>a<span>]</span></a></sup>

International media, including the media in the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union"), covered [racial discrimination in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_in_the_United_States "Racism in the United States")<sup id="cite_ref-Journal_136-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Journal-136"><span>[</span>135<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-137"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-137"><span>[</span>136<span>]</span></a></sup>

In a meeting with President [Harry Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Truman "Harry Truman") in 1946, [Paul Robeson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Robeson "Paul Robeson") urged him to take action against lynching. In 1951, Robeson and the [Civil Rights Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Congress "Civil Rights Congress") made a presentation entitled "[We Charge Genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_Charge_Genocide "We Charge Genocide")" to the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations "United Nations") a document which argued that the U.S. government's failure to act against lynchings meant it was guilty of [genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide "Genocide") under Article II of the [United Nations Genocide Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_the_Prevention_and_Punishment_of_the_Crime_of_Genocide "Convention on the Prevention and Punishment of the Crime of Genocide"). Based on his commissions report _To Secure There Rights_, Truman introduced in Congress the first presidentially proposed comprehensive Civil Rights Bill - it included legislation making lynching a federal crime. The first year on record with no lynchings reported in the United States was 1952.<sup id="cite_ref-138"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-138"><span>[</span>137<span>]</span></a></sup>

In the early [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War "Cold War") years, the FBI was worried more about possible communist connections among anti-lynching groups than about the lynching crimes. For instance, the FBI branded [Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein "Albert Einstein") a communist sympathizer for joining Robeson's [American Crusade Against Lynching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Crusade_Against_Lynching "American Crusade Against Lynching").<sup id="cite_ref-einstein_139-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-einstein-139"><span>[</span>138<span>]</span></a></sup>

### Civil rights movement

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=24 "Edit section: Civil rights movement")\]

By the 1950s, the [civil rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_rights_movement "Civil rights movement") was gaining momentum. Membership in the NAACP increased in states across the country. The NAACP achieved [a significant U.S. Supreme Court victory in 1954 ruling that segregated education was unconstitutional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_v._Board_of_Education "Brown v. Board of Education"). A 1955 lynching that sparked public outrage about injustice was that of [Emmett Till](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmett_Till "Emmett Till"), a 14-year-old boy from Chicago. Spending the summer with relatives in [Money, Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Money,_Mississippi "Money, Mississippi"), Till was killed by a mob of white men. Till had been badly beaten, one of his eyes was gouged out, and he was shot in the head before being thrown into the [Tallahatchie River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tallahatchie_River "Tallahatchie River"), his body weighed down with a 70-pound (32 kg) [cotton gin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_gin "Cotton gin") fan tied around his neck with barbed wire. His mother insisted on a public funeral with an open casket, to show people how badly Till's body had been disfigured. News photographs circulated around the country, and drew intense public reaction. The visceral response to his mother's decision to have an open-casket funeral mobilized the Black community throughout the U.S.<sup id="cite_ref-Atlantic_140-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Atlantic-140"><span>[</span>139<span>]</span></a></sup> The state of Mississippi tried two defendants, but they were speedily acquitted by an [all-white jury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-white_jury "All-white jury").<sup id="cite_ref-141"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-141"><span>[</span>140<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-142"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-142"><span>[</span>141<span>]</span></a></sup> [Carolyn Bryant Donham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolyn_Bryant_Donham "Carolyn Bryant Donham") has since come out and publicly admitted in an interview with Timothy Tyson to falsely accusing Emmett Till.

In the 1960s, the civil rights movement attracted students to the South from all over the country to work on voter registration and integration. The intervention of people from outside the communities and threat of social change aroused fear and resentment among many Whites. In June 1964, [three civil rights workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murders_of_Chaney,_Goodman,_and_Schwerner "Murders of Chaney, Goodman, and Schwerner") disappeared in [Neshoba County, Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neshoba_County,_Mississippi "Neshoba County, Mississippi"). They had been investigating the arson of a Black church being used as a "[Freedom School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Schools "Freedom Schools")". Six weeks later, their bodies were found in a partially constructed dam near [Philadelphia, Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philadelphia,_Mississippi "Philadelphia, Mississippi"). [James Chaney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Chaney "James Chaney") of [Meridian, Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meridian,_Mississippi "Meridian, Mississippi"), and [Michael Schwerner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Schwerner "Michael Schwerner") and [Andrew Goodman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Goodman_(activist) "Andrew Goodman (activist)") of [New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City "New York City") had been members of the [Congress of Racial Equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Racial_Equality "Congress of Racial Equality"). They had been dedicated to non-violent direct action against [racial discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_discrimination "Racial discrimination"). The investigation also unearthed the bodies of numerous anonymous victims of past lynchings and murders.

The United States prosecuted 18 men for a [Ku Klux Klan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku_Klux_Klan "Ku Klux Klan") conspiracy to deprive the victims of their civil rights under 19th century federal law, in order to prosecute the crime in a federal court. Seven men were convicted but received light sentences, two men were released because of a deadlocked jury, and the remainder were acquitted. In 2005, 80-year-old [Edgar Ray Killen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgar_Ray_Killen "Edgar Ray Killen"), one of the men who had earlier gone free, was retried by the state of Mississippi, convicted of three counts of [manslaughter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manslaughter "Manslaughter") in a new trial, and sentenced to 60 years in [prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prison "Prison"). Killen died in 2018 after serving 12+1⁄2 years.

Because of J. Edgar Hoover's and others' hostility to the civil rights movement, agents of the FBI resorted to outright lying to smear civil rights workers and other opponents of lynching. For example, the FBI leaked false information in the press about the lynching victim [Viola Liuzzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viola_Liuzzo "Viola Liuzzo"), who was murdered in 1965 in Alabama. The FBI said Liuzzo had been a member of the [Communist Party USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_USA "Communist Party USA"), had abandoned her five children, and was involved in sexual relationships with African Americans in the movement.<sup id="cite_ref-liuzzosmear_143-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-liuzzosmear-143"><span>[</span>142<span>]</span></a></sup>

## After the civil rights movement

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=25 "Edit section: After the civil rights movement")\]

Although lynchings have become rare following the civil rights movement and the resulting changes in American social norms, some lynchings have still occurred. In 1981, two Klan members in [Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama "Alabama") randomly selected a 19-year-old Black man, [Michael Donald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Donald "Michael Donald"), and [murdered him](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_Michael_Donald "Lynching of Michael Donald"), in order to retaliate for a jury's acquittal of a Black man who was accused of murdering a white police officer. The Klansmen were caught, prosecuted, and convicted (one of the Klansmen, Henry Hayes, was sentenced to death and executed on June 6, 1997). A $7 million judgment in a civil suit against the Klan bankrupted the local Klan subgroup, the [United Klans of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Klans_of_America "United Klans of America").<sup id="cite_ref-donald_144-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-donald-144"><span>[</span>143<span>]</span></a></sup>

In 1998, Shawn Allen Berry, Lawrence Russel Brewer, and ex-convict John William King [murdered James Byrd Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_James_Byrd_Jr. "Murder of James Byrd Jr.") in [Jasper, Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jasper,_Texas "Jasper, Texas"). Byrd was a 49-year-old father of three, who had accepted an early-morning ride home with the three men. They attacked him and dragged him to his death behind their truck.<sup id="cite_ref-145"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-145"><span>[</span>144<span>]</span></a></sup> The three men dumped their victim's mutilated remains in the town's segregated African-American cemetery and then went to a barbecue.<sup id="cite_ref-146"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-146"><span>[</span>145<span>]</span></a></sup> Local authorities immediately treated the murder as a [hate crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_crime "Hate crime") and requested FBI assistance. The murderers (two of whom turned out to be members of a [white supremacist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_supremacy "White supremacy") [prison gang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prison_gang "Prison gang")) were caught and stood trial. Brewer and King were both sentenced to death (with Brewer being executed in 2011, and King being executed in 2019). Berry was sentenced to life in prison.

On June 13, 2005, the [U.S. Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate "United States Senate") formally apologized for its failure to enact a federal anti-lynching law in the early 20th century, "when it was most needed". Before the vote, Louisiana senator [Mary Landrieu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Landrieu "Mary Landrieu") noted: "There may be no other injustice in [American history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_United_States "History of the United States") for which the Senate so uniquely bears responsibility."<sup id="cite_ref-senate-apology_147-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-senate-apology-147"><span>[</span>146<span>]</span></a></sup> The resolution was passed on a voice vote with 80 senators cosponsoring, with Mississippians [Thad Cochran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thad_Cochran "Thad Cochran") and [Trent Lott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trent_Lott "Trent Lott") being among the 20 U.S. senators abstaining.<sup id="cite_ref-senate-apology_147-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-senate-apology-147"><span>[</span>146<span>]</span></a></sup> The resolution expressed "the deepest sympathies and most solemn regrets of the Senate to the descendants of victims of lynching, the ancestors of whom were deprived of life, human dignity and the constitutional protections accorded all citizens of the United States".<sup id="cite_ref-senate-apology_147-2"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-senate-apology-147"><span>[</span>146<span>]</span></a></sup>

In February 2014, a noose was placed on the statue of [James Meredith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Meredith "James Meredith"), the first African-American student at the [University of Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Mississippi "University of Mississippi").<sup id="cite_ref-148"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-148"><span>[</span>147<span>]</span></a></sup> A number of [nooses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noose "Noose") appeared in 2017, primarily in or near Washington, D.C.<sup id="cite_ref-149"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-149"><span>[</span>148<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-150"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-150"><span>[</span>149<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-151"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-151"><span>[</span>150<span>]</span></a></sup>

In August 2014, [Lennon Lacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Lennon_Lacy "Death of Lennon Lacy"), a teenager from [Bladenboro, North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bladenboro,_North_Carolina "Bladenboro, North Carolina"), who had been dating a white girl, was found dead, hanging from a swing set. His family believes that he was lynched, but the FBI stated, after an investigation, that it found no evidence of a [hate crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hate_crime "Hate crime"). The case is featured in a 2019 documentary about lynching in America, _Always in Season_.<sup id="cite_ref-152"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-152"><span>[</span>151<span>]</span></a></sup>

In May 2017, Mississippi Republican state representative [Karl Oliver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Oliver "Karl Oliver") of [Winona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winona,_Mississippi "Winona, Mississippi") stated that Louisiana lawmakers who supported the removal of Confederate monuments from their state should be lynched. Oliver's district includes Money, Mississippi, where [Emmett Till](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmett_Till "Emmett Till") was murdered in 1955. Mississippi's leaders from both the Republican and Democratic parties quickly condemned Oliver's statement.<sup id="cite_ref-153"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-153"><span>[</span>152<span>]</span></a></sup>

In 2018, the Senate attempted to pass new anti-lynching legislation (the [Justice for Victims of Lynching Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_for_Victims_of_Lynching_Act "Justice for Victims of Lynching Act")), but this failed.

On April 26, 2018, in [Montgomery, Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery,_Alabama "Montgomery, Alabama"), the [National Memorial for Peace and Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Memorial_for_Peace_and_Justice "National Memorial for Peace and Justice") opened. Founded by the [Equal Justice Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equal_Justice_Initiative "Equal Justice Initiative") of that city, it is the first memorial created specifically to document lynchings of African Americans.

In 2019, [Goodloe Sutton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodloe_Sutton "Goodloe Sutton"), then editor of a small Alabama newspaper, _[The Democrat-Reporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Democrat-Reporter "The Democrat-Reporter")_, received national publicity after he said in an editorial that the Ku Klux Klan was needed to "clean up D.C."<sup id="cite_ref-154"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-154"><span>[</span>153<span>]</span></a></sup> When he was asked what he meant by "cleaning up D.C.", he stated: "We'll get the hemp ropes out, loop them over a tall limb and hang all of them." "When he was asked if he felt that it was appropriate for the publisher of a newspaper to call for the lynching of [Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americans "Americans"), Sutton doubled down on his position by stating: …'It's not calling for the lynchings of Americans. These are [socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism "Socialism")\-[communists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism "Communism") we're talking about. Do you know what socialism and communism is?'" He denied that the Klan was a racist and violent organization, comparing it to the [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP "NAACP").<sup id="cite_ref-155"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-155"><span>[</span>154<span>]</span></a></sup>

On January 6, 2021, during the [2021 storming of the United States Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_storming_of_the_United_States_Capitol "2021 storming of the United States Capitol"), the rioters shouted "Hang [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence "Mike Pence")!" in an attempt to find and lynch the [vice president of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_vice_presidents_of_the_United_States "List of vice presidents of the United States") for [refusing to overturn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempts_to_overturn_the_2020_United_States_presidential_election "Attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election") the [2020 United States Presidential Election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_Presidential_Election "2020 United States Presidential Election") in favor of President [Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump"), and a rudimentary gallows was built on the Capitol lawn.<sup id="cite_ref-Pengelly_156-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Pengelly-156"><span>[</span>155<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-157"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-157"><span>[</span>156<span>]</span></a></sup>

As of 2021, the Equal Justice Initiative claims that lynchings never stopped, listing 8 deaths in Mississippi that law-enforcement deemed suicides, but the civil rights organization _Julian_ believes were lynchings. According to Jill Jefferson, "There is a pattern to how these cases are investigated", "When authorities arrive on the scene of a hanging, it's treated as a suicide almost immediately. The crime scene is not preserved. The investigation is shoddy. And then there is a formal ruling of suicide, despite evidence to the contrary. And the case is never heard from again unless someone brings it up."<sup id="cite_ref-Mississippi_never_stopped_158-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Mississippi_never_stopped-158"><span>[</span>157<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d3/Lynching-of-casey-and-cora.jpg/250px-Lynching-of-casey-and-cora.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lynching-of-casey-and-cora.jpg)

The 1856 lynching of Charles Cora and James Casey by the [Committee of Vigilance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Committee_of_Vigilance "San Francisco Committee of Vigilance") in [San Francisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco "San Francisco"), [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California "California")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Lynching_1882_by_AW_Piper.jpg/250px-Lynching_1882_by_AW_Piper.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lynching_1882_by_AW_Piper.jpg)

Illustration drawn by [A. W. Piper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._W._Piper "A. W. Piper") depicting the January 18, 1882, lynching of James Sullivan, William Howard, and Benjamin Payne in [Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle "Seattle"), [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_(state) "Washington (state)")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Hanging_of_Juanita_in_Downieville.jpg/250px-Hanging_of_Juanita_in_Downieville.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Hanging_of_Juanita_in_Downieville.jpg)

The hanging of [Josefa Segovia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josefa_Segovia "Josefa Segovia") (Juanita) in Downieville, 1851

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Lynching_of_Francisco_Arias_and_Jos%C3%A9_Chamales.jpg/250px-Lynching_of_Francisco_Arias_and_Jos%C3%A9_Chamales.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lynching_of_Francisco_Arias_and_Jos%C3%A9_Chamales.jpg)

Two Mexican-American men, Francisco Arias and José Chamales, lynched in [Santa Cruz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_California "Santa Cruz, California"), California, in 1877

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/09/Lynching_of_the_Ruggle_Brothers.jpg/250px-Lynching_of_the_Ruggle_Brothers.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Lynching_of_the_Ruggle_Brothers.jpg)

[Lynching of the Ruggles brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_the_Ruggles_brothers "Lynching of the Ruggles brothers") in [Redding, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redding,_California "Redding, California") on July 24, 1892

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/George_Witherell%2C_the_murderer_of_Wall_%26_McCain._Lynched_Dec._4%2C_1888_LCCN2012646362.jpg/250px-George_Witherell%2C_the_murderer_of_Wall_%26_McCain._Lynched_Dec._4%2C_1888_LCCN2012646362.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:George_Witherell,_the_murderer_of_Wall_%26_McCain._Lynched_Dec._4,_1888_LCCN2012646362.jpg)

Lifeless body of [George Witherell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_George_Witherell "Lynching of George Witherell") hanging from a telephone pole in [Cañon City, Colorado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ca%C3%B1on_City,_Colorado "Cañon City, Colorado"), after being lynched on December 4, 1888

Historians have debated the history of lynchings on the western frontier, which has been obscured by the [mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mythology "Mythology") of the [American Old West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_frontier "American frontier"). In unorganized territories or sparsely settled states, law enforcement was limited, often provided only by a [U.S. Marshal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Marshals_Service "United States Marshals Service") who might, despite the appointment of deputies, be hours, or days, away by horseback.

People often carried out lynchings in the Old West against accused criminals in custody. Lynching did not so much substitute for an absent legal system as constitute an alternative system dominated by a particular social class or racial group. Historian Michael J. Pfeifer writes, "Contrary to the popular understanding, early territorial lynching did not flow from an absence or distance of law enforcement but rather from the social instability of early communities and their contest for property, status, and the definition of social order."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPfeifer200430_159-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPfeifer200430-159"><span>[</span>158<span>]</span></a></sup>

The exact number of people in the Western states/territories killed in lynchings is unknown. There were 571 lynchings of Mexicans between 1848 and 1928. The most recorded deaths were in [Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas "Texas"), with up to 232 killings, then followed by [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California "California") (143 deaths), [New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico "New Mexico") (87 deaths), and [Arizona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arizona "Arizona") (48 deaths). Lynch mobs killed Mexicans for a variety of reasons, with the most common accusations being murder and robbery. Others were killed as suspected bandits or rebels.<sup id="cite_ref-carrigan_160-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-carrigan-160"><span>[</span>159<span>]</span></a></sup>

Many of the Mexicans who were native to what would become a state within the United States were experienced miners, and they had great success mining gold in California.<sup id="cite_ref-Carrigan&amp;Webb_30-3"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Carrigan&amp;Webb-30"><span>[</span>30<span>]</span></a></sup> Their success aroused animosity by white prospectors, who intimidated Mexican miners with the threat of violence and committed violence against some. Between 1848 and 1860, white Americans lynched at least 163 Mexicans in California.<sup id="cite_ref-Carrigan&amp;Webb_30-4"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Carrigan&amp;Webb-30"><span>[</span>30<span>]</span></a></sup> On July 5, 1851, a mob in [Downieville, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downieville,_California "Downieville, California"), lynched a Mexican woman named [Josefa Segovia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josefa_Segovia "Josefa Segovia").<sup id="cite_ref-161"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-161"><span>[</span>160<span>]</span></a></sup> She was accused of killing a white man who had attempted to assault her after breaking into her home.<sup id="cite_ref-loc_162-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-loc-162"><span>[</span>161<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-163"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-163"><span>[</span>162<span>]</span></a></sup>

The [San Francisco Vigilance Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Vigilance_Movement "San Francisco Vigilance Movement") has traditionally been portrayed as a positive response to government corruption and rampant crime, but revisionist historians have argued that it created more lawlessness than it eliminated.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPfeifer2004[[Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2014]]&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;[[Wikipedia:Citing_sources|&lt;span_title=&quot;This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(January_2014)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;]]&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;_164-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPfeifer2004[[Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2014]]%3Csup_class=%22noprint_Inline-Template_%22_style=%22white-space:nowrap;%22%3E&amp;#91;%3Ci%3E[[Wikipedia:Citing_sources|%3Cspan_title=%22This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(January_2014)%22%3Epage&amp;nbsp;needed%3C/span%3E]]%3C/i%3E&amp;#93;%3C/sup%3E-164"><span>[</span>163<span>]</span></a></sup> Four men were executed by the 1851 Committee of Vigilance before it disbanded. When the second Committee of Vigilance was instituted in 1856, in response to the murder of publisher [James King of William](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_King_of_William "James King of William"), it hanged a total of four men, all accused of murder.<sup id="cite_ref-165"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-165"><span>[</span>164<span>]</span></a></sup>

During the same year of 1851, just after the beginning of the [Gold Rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Gold_Rush "California Gold Rush"), these Committees lynched an unnamed thief in northern California. The Gold Rush and the economic prosperity of Mexican-born people was one of the main reasons for mob violence against them. Other factors include land and livestock, since they were also a form of economic success. In conjunction with lynching, mobs also attempted to expel Mexicans, and other groups such as the indigenous peoples of the region, from areas with great mining activity and gold. As a result of the violence against Mexicans, many formed bands of bandits and would raid towns. One case, in 1855, was when a group of bandits went into Rancheria and killed six people. When the news of this incident spread, a mob of 1,500 people formed, rounded up 38 Mexicans, and executed Puertanino.<sup>[<i><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too-vague attribution or weasel words. (September 2019)">who?</span></a></i>]</sup> The mob also expelled all the Mexicans in Rancheria and nearby towns as well, burning their homes.<sup id="cite_ref-166"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-166"><span>[</span>165<span>]</span></a></sup>

On October 24, 1871, a mob rampaged through [Old Chinatown, Los Angeles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Chinatown,_Los_Angeles "Old Chinatown, Los Angeles") and [killed at least 18 Chinese Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Chinese_massacre_of_1871 "Los Angeles Chinese massacre of 1871"), after a white businessman had inadvertently been killed there in the crossfire of a [tong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tong_(organization) "Tong (organization)") battle within the Chinese community.

After the body of [Brooke Hart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooke_Hart "Brooke Hart") was found on November 26, 1933, Thomas Harold Thurman and John Holmes, who had confessed to kidnapping and murdering Hart, were lynched on November 26 or November 27, 1933.<sup id="cite_ref-167"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-167"><span>[</span>166<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-168"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-168"><span>[</span>167<span>]</span></a></sup>

Four lynchings are included in a book of 12 [case studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_studies "Case studies") of the prosecution of homicides in the early years of statehood of [Minnesota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnesota "Minnesota").<sup id="cite_ref-169"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-169"><span>[</span>168<span>]</span></a></sup> In 1858 the institutions of justice in the new state, such as jails, were scarce and the lynching of Charles J. Reinhart at [Lexington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexington_Township,_Le_Sueur_County,_Minnesota "Lexington Township, Le Sueur County, Minnesota") on December 19 was not prosecuted. The following year, the lynching of Oscar Jackson at [Minnehaha Falls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minnehaha_Falls "Minnehaha Falls") on April 25 resulted in a later charging of Aymer Moore for the homicide.

In 1866, when Alexander Campbell and George Liscomb were lynched at [Mankato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mankato "Mankato") on Christmas Day, the state returned an indictment of John Gut on September 18 the following year. Then in 1872 when Bobolink, a Native American, was accused and imprisoned pending trial in [Saint Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Paul,_Minnesota "Saint Paul, Minnesota"), the state avoided his lynching despite a public outcry against Natives.

"Lynching in Texas", a project of [Sam Houston State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Houston_State_University "Sam Houston State University"), maintains a database of more than 600 lynchings committed in Texas between 1882 and 1942.<sup id="cite_ref-170"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-170"><span>[</span>169<span>]</span></a></sup> Many of the lynchings were of people of Mexican heritage.

During the early 1900s, hostilities between Whites and Mexicans along the "Brown Belt" were common. In [Rocksprings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocksprings,_Texas "Rocksprings, Texas"), Antonio Rodriguez, a Mexican, was burned at the stake. This event was widely publicized and protests against the treatment of Mexicans in the U.S. erupted within the interior of Mexico, namely in [Guadalajara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadalajara "Guadalajara") and Mexico City.<sup id="cite_ref-171"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-171"><span>[</span>170<span>]</span></a></sup>

Members of the [Texas Rangers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texas_Ranger_Division "Texas Ranger Division") were charged in 1918 with the murder of Florencio Garcia. Two rangers had taken Garcia into custody for a theft investigation. The next day they let Garcia go, and were last seen escorting him on a mule. Garcia was never seen again. A month after the interrogation, bones and Garcia's clothing were found beside the road where the Rangers claimed to have let Garcia go. The Rangers were arrested for murder, freed on bail, and acquitted due to lack of evidence. The case became part of the [Canales investigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canales_investigation "Canales investigation") into criminal conduct by the Rangers.<sup id="cite_ref-Utley_172-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Utley-172"><span>[</span>171<span>]</span></a></sup><sup><span title="Page / location: 80">: 80 </span></sup> 

In 1859, white settlers began to expel Mexicans from Arizona. The mob was able to chase Mexicans out of many towns, southward. Even though they were successful in doing so, the mob followed and killed many of the people that had been chased out. The Sonoita massacre was a result of these expulsions, where white settlers killed four Mexicans and one Native American.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPfeifer2013[httpswwwgooglecombookseditionLynching_Beyond_DixieCR0TMCIfE14Chlengbpv1dqSonoitafourMexicansoneNativeAmericanpgPA115printsecfrontcover_115]_173-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPfeifer2013[httpswwwgooglecombookseditionLynching_Beyond_DixieCR0TMCIfE14Chlengbpv1dqSonoitafourMexicansoneNativeAmericanpgPA115printsecfrontcover_115]-173"><span>[</span>172<span>]</span></a></sup>

On April 19, 1915, the Leon brothers were lynched by hanging in [Pima Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pima_County,_Arizona "Pima County, Arizona") by deputies Robert Fenter and Frank Moore. This lynching was unusual, since the perpetrators were arrested, tried, and convicted for the murders. Fenter and Moore were both found guilty of second degree murder and each sentenced to 10 years to life in prison. However, they were both pardoned by Governor [George W. P. Hunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._P._Hunt "George W. P. Hunt") upon his departure from office in February 1917.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPfeifer2013[[Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2023]]&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;[[Wikipedia:Citing_sources|&lt;span_title=&quot;Page_number_previously_given_as_366,_which_is_the_number_of_pages_in_the_book,_and_not_a_valid_page_number_for_content.&amp;#32;(January_2023)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;]]&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;_174-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPfeifer2013[[Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_January_2023]]%3Csup_class=%22noprint_Inline-Template_%22_style=%22white-space:nowrap;%22%3E&amp;#91;%3Ci%3E[[Wikipedia:Citing_sources|%3Cspan_title=%22Page_number_previously_given_as_366,_which_is_the_number_of_pages_in_the_book,_and_not_a_valid_page_number_for_content.&amp;#32;(January_2023)%22%3Epage&amp;nbsp;needed%3C/span%3E]]%3C/i%3E&amp;#93;%3C/sup%3E-174"><span>[</span>173<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-175"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-175"><span>[</span>174<span>]</span></a></sup>

Another well-documented episode in the history of the American West is the [Johnson County War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson_County_War "Johnson County War"), a dispute in the 1890s over land use in [Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming "Wyoming"). Large-scale ranchers hired [mercenaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercenary "Mercenary") to lynch the small ranchers.

[Alonzo Tucker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alonzo_Tucker_(lynching_victim) "Alonzo Tucker (lynching victim)") was a traveling boxer who was heading north from California to Washington. Part of his travels led him to stay in [Coos Bay, Oregon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coos_Bay,_Oregon "Coos Bay, Oregon"), where he was lynched by a mob on September 18, 1902. He was accused by Mrs. Dennis for assault. After the lynching, Dennis and her family quickly left town and headed to California. Tucker is the only documented lynching of a Black man in Oregon.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJensen2008[[Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_September_2020]]&lt;sup_class=&quot;noprint_Inline-Template_&quot;_style=&quot;white-space:nowrap;&quot;&gt;&amp;#91;&lt;i&gt;[[Wikipedia:Citing_sources|&lt;span_title=&quot;This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(September_2020)&quot;&gt;page&amp;nbsp;needed&lt;/span&gt;]]&lt;/i&gt;&amp;#93;&lt;/sup&gt;_176-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJensen2008[[Category:Wikipedia_articles_needing_page_number_citations_from_September_2020]]%3Csup_class=%22noprint_Inline-Template_%22_style=%22white-space:nowrap;%22%3E&amp;#91;%3Ci%3E[[Wikipedia:Citing_sources|%3Cspan_title=%22This_citation_requires_a_reference_to_the_specific_page_or_range_of_pages_in_which_the_material_appears.&amp;#32;(September_2020)%22%3Epage&amp;nbsp;needed%3C/span%3E]]%3C/i%3E&amp;#93;%3C/sup%3E-176"><span>[</span>175<span>]</span></a></sup>
