Other lynchings include many Native Americans.<sup id="cite_ref-177"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-177"><span>[</span>176<span>]</span></a></sup>

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/NAACP_Anti-lynching_broadside.jpg/250px-NAACP_Anti-lynching_broadside.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:NAACP_Anti-lynching_broadside.jpg)

Anti-lynching broadside by the [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP "NAACP") stating "The United States is the Only Land on Earth where Human Beings are Burned at the Stake"

For most of the history of the United States, lynching was rarely prosecuted, as the same people who would have had to prosecute and sit on juries were generally on the side of the action or related to the perpetrators in the small communities where many lived. When the crime was prosecuted, it was under state murder statutes. In one example in 1907–09, the U.S. Supreme Court tried its only criminal case in history, [203](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_Supreme_Court_cases,_volume_203 "List of United States Supreme Court cases, volume 203") [U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Reports "United States Reports") [563](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/203/563/) ([_U.S. v. Sheriff Shipp_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Shipp "United States v. Shipp")). Shipp was found guilty of criminal contempt for doing nothing to stop the mob in [Chattanooga, Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chattanooga,_Tennessee "Chattanooga, Tennessee") that lynched [Ed Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_Ed_Johnson "Lynching of Ed Johnson"), who was in jail for rape.<sup id="cite_ref-Supreme_Court_opinion_in_U.S._vs._Shipp_178-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Supreme_Court_opinion_in_U.S._vs._Shipp-178"><span>[</span>177<span>]</span></a></sup> In the South, Blacks generally were not able to serve on juries, as they could not vote, having been [disfranchised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement_after_Reconstruction_era "Disfranchisement after Reconstruction era") by discriminatory voter registration and electoral rules passed by majority-white legislatures in the late 19th century, who also imposed [Jim Crow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow "Jim Crow") laws.

Starting in 1909, federal legislators introduced more than 200 bills in Congress to make lynching a federal crime, but they failed to pass, chiefly because of Southern legislators' opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-179"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-179"><span>[</span>178<span>]</span></a></sup> Because Southern states had effectively [disfranchised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement_after_Reconstruction_era "Disfranchisement after Reconstruction era") African Americans at the start of the 20th century, the white Southern Democrats controlled all the apportioned seats of the South, nearly double the Congressional representation that white residents alone would have been entitled to. They were a powerful voting bloc for decades and controlled important committee chairmanships. The Senate Democrats formed a bloc that filibustered for a week in December 1922, holding up all national business, to defeat the [Dyer Anti-Lynching Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dyer_Anti-Lynching_Bill "Dyer Anti-Lynching Bill"). It had passed the House in January 1922 with broad support except for the South. Representative [Leonidas C. Dyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonidas_C._Dyer "Leonidas C. Dyer") of [St. Louis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Louis "St. Louis"), the chief sponsor, undertook a national speaking tour in support of the bill in 1923, but the Southern Senators defeated it twice more in the next two sessions.

Under the [Franklin D. Roosevelt administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Franklin_D._Roosevelt "Presidency of Franklin D. Roosevelt"), the [Civil Rights Section of the Justice Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Justice_Civil_Rights_Division "United States Department of Justice Civil Rights Division") tried, but failed, to prosecute lynchers under Reconstruction era civil rights laws. The first successful federal prosecution of a lyncher for a civil rights violation was in 1946. By that time, the era of lynchings as a common occurrence had ended. [Adam Clayton Powell Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Clayton_Powell_Jr. "Adam Clayton Powell Jr.") succeeded in gaining House passage of an anti-lynching bill, but it was defeated in the Senate, still dominated by the Southern Democratic bloc, supported by its disfranchisement of Blacks.

On December 19, 2018, the U.S. Senate voted unanimously in favor of the "[Justice for Victims of Lynching Act of 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_for_Victims_of_Lynching_Act_of_2018 "Justice for Victims of Lynching Act of 2018")" which, for the first time in U.S. history, would make lynching a federal hate crime.<sup id="cite_ref-180"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-180"><span>[</span>179<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Egwuonwu_181-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Egwuonwu-181"><span>[</span>180<span>]</span></a></sup> The legislation had been reintroduced to the Senate earlier that year as Senate Bill S. 3178 by the three African-American U.S. senators, [Tim Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Scott "Tim Scott"), [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris "Kamala Harris"), and [Cory Booker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cory_Booker "Cory Booker").<sup id="cite_ref-182"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-182"><span>[</span>181<span>]</span></a></sup> As of June 2019 the bill, which failed to become law during the [115th U.S. Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/115th_United_States_Congress "115th United States Congress"), had been reintroduced as the [Emmett Till Antilynching Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmett_Till_Antilynching_Act "Emmett Till Antilynching Act"). The House of Representatives voted 410–4 to pass it on February 26, 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-183"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-183"><span>[</span>182<span>]</span></a></sup>

As of June 4, 2020, while protests and civil unrest over the [murder of George Floyd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_George_Floyd "Murder of George Floyd") were occurring nationwide, the bill was being considered by the Senate, with Senator [Rand Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rand_Paul "Rand Paul") preventing the bill from being passed by unanimous consent. Paul opposed the passage of the bill because, according to Paul, the language was overly broad, included attacks which he believed were not extreme enough to qualify as "lynching". He stated that "this bill would cheapen the meaning of lynching by defining it so broadly as to include a minor bruise or abrasion," and he has proposed the inclusion of an amendment that would apply a "serious bodily injury standard" for a crime to be classified as a lynching.<sup id="cite_ref-Foran2020_184-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Foran2020-184"><span>[</span>183<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-185"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-185"><span>[</span>184<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Barrett2020_186-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Barrett2020-186"><span>[</span>185<span>]</span></a></sup>

House Majority Leader [Steny Hoyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steny_Hoyer "Steny Hoyer") criticized Rand Paul's position, saying on Twitter that "it is shameful that one GOP Senator is standing in the way of seeing this bill become law." Senator [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris "Kamala Harris") added that "Senator Paul is now trying to weaken a bill that was already passed – there's no reason for this" while speaking to have the amendment defeated.<sup id="cite_ref-Barrett2020_186-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Barrett2020-186"><span>[</span>185<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Foran2020_184-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Foran2020-184"><span>[</span>183<span>]</span></a></sup>

In 2022, the [Emmett Till Anti-Lynching Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmett_Till_Antilynching_Act "Emmett Till Antilynching Act") passed both chambers of Congress and [President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_United_States "President of the United States") [Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden "Joe Biden") signed it into federal law on March 29 of that year.<sup id="cite_ref-187"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-187"><span>[</span>186<span>]</span></a></sup> The Act, proposed by Illinois House Representative [Bobby L. Rush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Rush "Bobby Rush"), is the first bill to make lynching a federal hate crime, despite more than a century of failed attempts to pass an anti-lynching bill through Congress. Anyone who violates the terms of the bill will be penalized with a fine, a prison sentence of up to 30 years, or both.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_98-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-:1-98"><span>[</span>98<span>]</span></a></sup>

In 1933, California defined lynching, punishable by 2–4 years in prison, as "the taking by means of a riot of any person from the lawful custody of any peace officer", with the crime of "riot" defined as two or more people using violence or the threat of violence.<sup id="cite_ref-cbsnews.com_188-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-cbsnews.com-188"><span>[</span>187<span>]</span></a></sup> It does not refer to lynching homicide, and has been used to charge individuals who have tried to free someone in police custody – leading to controversy.<sup id="cite_ref-189"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-189"><span>[</span>188<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-190"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-190"><span>[</span>189<span>]</span></a></sup> In 2015, Governor [Jerry Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerry_Brown "Jerry Brown") signed legislation by Senator [Holly Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holly_Mitchell "Holly Mitchell") removing the word "lynching" from the state's criminal code without comment after it received unanimous approval in a vote by state lawmakers. Mitchell stated, "It's been said that strong words should be reserved for strong concepts, and 'lynching' has such a painful history for African Americans that the law should only use it for what it is – murder by mob." The law was otherwise unchanged.<sup id="cite_ref-cbsnews.com_188-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-cbsnews.com-188"><span>[</span>187<span>]</span></a></sup>

In 1899, Indiana passed anti-lynching legislation. It was enforced by Governor [Winfield T. Durbin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winfield_T._Durbin "Winfield T. Durbin"), who forced investigation into a 1902 lynching and removed the sheriff responsible. In 1903 he sent militia to bring order to a race riot which had broken out on [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_(United_States) "Independence Day (United States)") in [Evansville, Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evansville,_Indiana "Evansville, Indiana"). Durbin also publicly declared that an African-American man accused of murder was entitled to a fair trial.

In 1920, 600 men attempted to remove a Black prisoner from [Marion County Jail](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Marion_County_Jail&action=edit&redlink=1 "Marion County Jail (page does not exist)"), but were prevented by the city's police.<sup id="cite_ref-Bodenhamer_191-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Bodenhamer-191"><span>[</span>190<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Madison_192-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Madison-192"><span>[</span>191<span>]</span></a></sup> Lawrence Beitler photographed the [lynching of Thomas Shipp and Abram Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_of_Thomas_Shipp_and_Abram_Smith "Lynching of Thomas Shipp and Abram Smith") in 1930 in [Marion, Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marion,_Indiana "Marion, Indiana").<sup id="cite_ref-193"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-193"><span>[</span>192<span>]</span></a></sup> Seeing this image inspired [Abel Meeropol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Meeropol "Abel Meeropol") to write the song "[Strange Fruit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strange_Fruit "Strange Fruit")",<sup id="cite_ref-Blair_(2012)_194-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Blair_(2012)-194"><span>[</span>193<span>]</span></a></sup> which was popularized by singer [Billie Holiday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billie_Holiday "Billie Holiday"). In reaction to these murders, [Flossie Bailey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flossie_Bailey "Flossie Bailey") pushed for passage of the 1931 Indiana anti-lynching law.<sup id="cite_ref-Kaplan_195-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Kaplan-195"><span>[</span>194<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Thornbrough_(2000)_196-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Thornbrough_(2000)-196"><span>[</span>195<span>]</span></a></sup> The law provided for the immediate dismissal of any sheriff who allowed a jailed person to be lynched, and allowed the victim's family to sue for $10,000. However, local authorities failed to prosecute mob leaders. In one case, when a sheriff was indicted by Indiana's attorney general, James Ogden, the jury refused to convict.<sup id="cite_ref-Bodenhamer_191-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Bodenhamer-191"><span>[</span>190<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Madison_192-1"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Madison-192"><span>[</span>191<span>]</span></a></sup>

Virginia passed anti-lynching legislation, which was signed on March 14, 1928 by Governor [Harry F. Byrd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_F._Byrd "Harry F. Byrd") following a media campaign by _Norfolk Virginian-Pilot_ editor [Louis Isaac Jaffe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Isaac_Jaffe "Louis Isaac Jaffe") against mob violence.<sup id="cite_ref-197"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-197"><span>[</span>196<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-198"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-198"><span>[</span>197<span>]</span></a></sup>

In an odd turn in 1951, South Carolina passed a law criminalizing second-degree lynching, which it defined as "any act of violence inflicted by a mob upon the body of another person and from which death does not result shall constitute the crime of lynching in the second degree and shall be a felony. Any person found guilty of lynching in the second degree shall be confined at hard labor in the State Penitentiary for a term not exceeding 20 years nor less than three years, at the discretion of the presiding judge."<sup id="cite_ref-South_Carolina_law_199-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-South_Carolina_law-199"><span>[</span>198<span>]</span></a></sup> By 2003, however, all but two of the state's 46 counties charged blacks with second-degree lynching out of proportion to their representation in the population. In the prior 5 years, 4,000 adults were charged, and 136 were convicted. Black suspects were convicted of this assault charge at twice the rate of white suspects. 1,400 juvenile lynching charges were filed, and, in 2002, 231 Black youths were convicted, ten times as many as white youths.<sup id="cite_ref-200"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-200"><span>[</span>199<span>]</span></a></sup> In 2006, five white teenagers were given various sentences for second-degree lynching in a non-lethal attack on a young Black man in South Carolina.<sup id="cite_ref-Gaffney_Ledger_201-0"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-Gaffney_Ledger-201"><span>[</span>200<span>]</span></a></sup> In 2010, the South Carolina Sentencing Reform Commission voted to rename the law "assault and battery by a mob", and to soften consequences for situations in which no one was killed or seriously injured in an attack by two or more people on a single victim.<sup id="cite_ref-202"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-202"><span>[</span>201<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-203"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-203"><span>[</span>202<span>]</span></a></sup>

## Representation in popular culture

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=36 "Edit section: Representation in popular culture")\]

The [1935 New York anti-lynching exhibitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1935_New_York_anti-lynching_exhibitions "1935 New York anti-lynching exhibitions") were held in support of the [Costigan-Wagner Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costigan-Wagner_Bill "Costigan-Wagner Bill"), with many artworks depicting lynching in various ways.

_[Lynch Fragments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynch_Fragments "Lynch Fragments")_ is a series of abstract metal sculptures begun in 1963 by artist [Melvin Edwards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melvin_Edwards "Melvin Edwards"), who has described the works as metaphors for both the violence inflicted on black people in the United States, and the power and struggles of African Americans fighting against that violence. The title of the first work in the series, _Some Bright Morning_, references an account in [Ralph Ginzburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Ginzburg "Ralph Ginzburg")'s 1962 anthology _100 Years of Lynchings_.<sup id="cite_ref-204"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-204"><span>[</span>203<span>]</span></a></sup>

### Literature and films

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Lynching_in_the_United_States&action=edit&section=37 "Edit section: Literature and films")\]

-   [Mark Twain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Twain "Mark Twain")'s _[Adventures of Huckleberry Finn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adventures_of_Huckleberry_Finn "Adventures of Huckleberry Finn")_, an 1885 novel, depicts attempted (although failed) lynching as a minor episode.
-   [Owen Wister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Owen_Wister "Owen Wister")'s _[The Virginian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Virginian_(novel) "The Virginian (novel)")_, a 1902 seminal novel in the genre of [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Old_West "American Old West") novels in the United States, dealt with a fictional treatment of the [Johnson County War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson_County_War "Johnson County War") and frontier lynchings in the West.
-   _[Tracked by Bloodhounds; or, A Lynching at Cripple Creek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tracked_by_Bloodhounds;_or,_A_Lynching_at_Cripple_Creek "Tracked by Bloodhounds; or, A Lynching at Cripple Creek")_, a 1904 [silent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silent_film "Silent film") [crime drama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_film "Crime film") [short film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short_film "Short film") directed by [Harry Buckwalter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Buckwalter "Harry Buckwalter").
-   [Angelina Weld Grimké](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angelina_Weld_Grimk%C3%A9 "Angelina Weld Grimké")'s _[Rachel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_(play) "Rachel (play)")_ (1914) was the first play about the toll of racial violence directed at African-American families; it was produced in 1916.
-   Following the commercial and critical success of [D. W. Griffith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D._W._Griffith "D. W. Griffith")'s film, _Birth of a Nation_ (1915), which glorified the [Ku Klux Klan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku_Klux_Klan "Ku Klux Klan") for its violence during [Reconstruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_era_(United_States) "Reconstruction era (United States)"), African-American director and writer [Oscar Micheaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oscar_Micheaux "Oscar Micheaux") responded in 1919 with the film _[Within Our Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Within_Our_Gates "Within Our Gates")_. The climax of the film is the lynching of a Black family after one member of the family is wrongly accused of murder. Considered a commercial failure, the film was an inductee of the 1992 [National Film Registry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Film_Registry "National Film Registry") list.<sup id="cite_ref-205"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-205"><span>[</span>204<span>]</span></a></sup>
-   [Regina M. Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regina_M._Anderson "Regina M. Anderson")'s play, _Climbing Jacob's Ladder,_ was about a lynching; it was performed<sup>[<i><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers"><span title="The time period mentioned near this tag is ambiguous. (June 2018)">when?</span></a></i>]</sup> by the Krigwa Players (later called the Negro Experimental Theater), a Harlem theatre company.
-   [William Faulkner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Faulkner "William Faulkner")'s short story "[Dry September](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dry_September "Dry September")" (1931) tells the story of a lynch mob.
-   [Lynd Ward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynd_Ward "Lynd Ward")'s 1932 book _Wild Pilgrimage_ (printed in woodblock prints, with no text) includes three prints of the lynching of several Black men.
-   [Annie Nathan Meyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annie_Nathan_Meyer "Annie Nathan Meyer")'s 1932 play _[Black Souls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Souls_(play) "Black Souls (play)")_ was about an innocent black man lynched on the campus of a fictional college in the Deep South. It was performed on Broadway at the [Provincetown Playhouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provincetown_Playhouse "Provincetown Playhouse") in a cast led by [Juano Hernandez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juano_Hernandez "Juano Hernandez") and [Rose McClendon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_McClendon "Rose McClendon").<sup id="cite_ref-206"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-206"><span>[</span>205<span>]</span></a></sup>
-   In [Irving Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irving_Berlin "Irving Berlin")'s 1933 musical, _[As Thousands Cheer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/As_Thousands_Cheer "As Thousands Cheer"),_ [Ethel Waters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethel_Waters "Ethel Waters") sang a ballad about lynching, "[Supper Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supper_Time "Supper Time")". She wrote in her 1951 autobiography, _His Eye Was on the Sparrow_: "if one song could tell the story of an entire race, that was it."
-   _[Murder in Harlem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_in_Harlem "Murder in Harlem")_ (1935), by director [Oscar Micheaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oscar_Micheaux "Oscar Micheaux"), was one of three films he made based on events in the controversial trial of [Leo Frank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Frank "Leo Frank"), a northern Jewish man convicted of murder of a Georgia factory girl. He portrayed the character analogous to Frank as guilty and set the film in New York, removing sectional conflict as one of the cultural forces in the trial. Micheaux's first version was a silent film, _The Gunsaulus Mystery_ (1921). _Lem Hawkins Confession_ (1935) was also related to the Leo Frank trial.<sup id="cite_ref-207"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-207"><span>[</span>206<span>]</span></a></sup>
-   [John Steinbeck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Steinbeck "John Steinbeck")'s short story "The Vigilante" (1936) is retrospectively concerned with a lynching as seen by one of the chief participants in it. The story is based on historical events, namely the [1933 lynchings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooke_Hart "Brooke Hart") of John Maurice Holmes and Thomas Harold Thurmond in San Jose, California, on November 16, 1933.<sup id="cite_ref-208"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-208"><span>[</span>207<span>]</span></a></sup>
-   The film _[They Won't Forget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/They_Won%27t_Forget "They Won't Forget")_ (1937) was inspired by the Frank case; it featured the Leo Frank character portrayed as a [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christians "Christians").
-   In _[Fury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fury_(1936_film) "Fury (1936 film)")_ (1936), the German expatriate [Fritz Lang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritz_Lang "Fritz Lang") depicts a lynch mob burning down a jail in which Joe Wilson (played by Spencer Tracy) was held as a suspect in a kidnapping, a crime for which Wilson was soon after cleared. Lang had left Germany after the Nazis came to power. The story was based on a [1933 lynching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooke_Hart "Brooke Hart") in [San Jose, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Jose,_California "San Jose, California"). This had been recorded on [newsreel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsreel "Newsreel") footage and was an event in which [Governor of California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_California "Governor of California") [James Rolph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Rolph "James Rolph") refused to intervene.
-   In [Walter Van Tilburg Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Van_Tilburg_Clark "Walter Van Tilburg Clark")'s 1940 novel, _[The Ox-Bow Incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ox-Bow_Incident_(novel) "The Ox-Bow Incident (novel)")_, two drifters are drawn into a Western posse formed to find the murderer of a local man. After suspicion centered on three innocent cattle [rustlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle_raiding "Cattle raiding"), they were lynched, an injustice that deeply affected the drifters. The novel was adapted as a [1943 film by the same name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ox-Bow_Incident "The Ox-Bow Incident"). It symbolized a wartime defense of United States' values, seen to be based on law, versus the characterization of [Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany "Nazi Germany") as mob rule.
-   [Harper Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harper_Lee "Harper Lee")'s novel, _[To Kill a Mockingbird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/To_Kill_a_Mockingbird "To Kill a Mockingbird")_ (1960), featured a Black man, Tom Robinson, who is wrongfully accused of rape and narrowly escapes lynching. After having been wrongfully convicted by an all-white jury, Robinson is later killed while attempting to escape from prison. The novel was adapted as a [1962 film of the same name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/To_Kill_a_Mockingbird_(film) "To Kill a Mockingbird (film)") starring [Gregory Peck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregory_Peck "Gregory Peck").
-   "[Going to Meet the Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Going_to_Meet_the_Man_(short_story) "Going to Meet the Man (short story)")" (1965) is a short story by [James Baldwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Baldwin "James Baldwin") that includes an account of a particularly horrific lynching.
-   The 1968 film _[Hang 'Em High](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hang_%27Em_High "Hang 'Em High"),_ set on the Western frontier, stars [Clint Eastwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clint_Eastwood "Clint Eastwood").
-   The 1988 film _[Mississippi Burning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Burning "Mississippi Burning")_ includes a depiction of a Black man being lynched.
-   [Peter Matthiessen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Matthiessen "Peter Matthiessen") depicted several lynchings in his _Killing Mr. Watson_ trilogy (first volume published in 1990), set in Florida of the late 19th century.<sup id="cite_ref-209"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-209"><span>[</span>208<span>]</span></a></sup>
-   In the 1996 [made-for-television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_film "Television film") film _[Stolen Memories: Secrets from the Rose Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolen_Memories:_Secrets_from_the_Rose_Garden "Stolen Memories: Secrets from the Rose Garden")_, protagonist Jessyca ([Mary Tyler Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Tyler_Moore "Mary Tyler Moore")) slowly recalls scenes from the night in which she suffered severe and permanent [brain damage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brain_damage "Brain damage"), which include the lynching of her African American friend Isaac at the hands of the [Ku Klux Klan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku_Klux_Klan "Ku Klux Klan"), as well as her cousin Cletis's involvement.
-   "[A Party Down at the Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Party_Down_at_the_Square "A Party Down at the Square")" (first published in 1997) is a short story by [Ralph Ellison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Ellison "Ralph Ellison") that describes a lynching from the point of view of a white boy from Cincinnati.<sup id="cite_ref-210"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-210"><span>[</span>209<span>]</span></a></sup>
-   _[Vendetta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vendetta_(1999_film) "Vendetta (1999 film)")_, a 1999 [HBO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_Box_Office "Home Box Office") film starring [Christopher Walken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Walken "Christopher Walken") and directed by [Nicholas Meyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_Meyer "Nicholas Meyer"), is based on events that took place in [New Orleans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Orleans "New Orleans") in 1891. After the acquittal of 18 Italian American men falsely accused of the murder of police chief [David Hennessy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hennessy "David Hennessy"), a lynch mob attacked them, killing 11 by shooting or hanging in one of the largest mass lynchings in United States history.
-   [Jason Robert Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Robert_Brown "Jason Robert Brown")'s musical _[Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parade_(musical) "Parade (musical)")_ tells the story of [Leo Frank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Frank "Leo Frank"), a Jewish man lynched near [Atlanta, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlanta,_Georgia "Atlanta, Georgia") in the early 1900s after being convicted of murder of a young factory girl in a highly biased trial.
-   The 2014 biopic _[Get on Up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Get_on_Up_(film) "Get on Up (film)")_ about the life of American singer [James Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Brown "James Brown") features a scene in which a young Brown finds the body of a lynched man hanging from a tree near Brown's childhood home.
-   [Quentin Tarantino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quentin_Tarantino "Quentin Tarantino")'s film _[The Hateful Eight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hateful_Eight "The Hateful Eight")_ (2015), set in the [Reconstruction era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconstruction_era "Reconstruction era"), features a finale with a detailed depiction of the lynching of a white woman identified as a working-class racist Southerner, with graphic focus on her suffering, prompting some debate among critics about whether it is a political commentary on racism and hate in America or simply sensational and sexist exploitation.<sup id="cite_ref-211"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-211"><span>[</span>210<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-212"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-212"><span>[</span>211<span>]</span></a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-213"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-213"><span>[</span>212<span>]</span></a></sup>

Among artistic works that grappled with lynching was the song "Strange Fruit", written as a poem by [Abel Meeropol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Meeropol "Abel Meeropol") in 1939 and recorded by [Billie Holiday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billie_Holiday "Billie Holiday"). In part it goes:

> Southern trees bear a strange fruit,  
> Blood on the leaves and blood at the root,  
> Black bodies swinging in the Southern breeze,  
> Strange fruit hanging from the poplar trees.

The song became identified with her and was one of her most popular ones. The song became an anthem for the anti-lynching movement. It also contributed to activism of the American [civil rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_rights_movement "Civil rights movement"). A documentary about a lynching and the effects of protest songs and art, entitled _Strange Fruit_ (2002) and produced by Public Broadcasting Service, was aired on U.S. television.<sup id="cite_ref-214"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-214"><span>[</span>213<span>]</span></a></sup>

1.  **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_ref-135 "Jump up")** Wade, 1987, gave a similar account, but suggested that the meeting was a regular Klan one.<sup id="cite_ref-132"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-132"><span>[</span>132<span>]</span></a></sup> An interview with Truman's friend Hinde at the Truman Library's web site portrayed the meeting as one-on-one at the Hotel Baltimore with a Klan organizer named Jones.<sup id="cite_ref-133"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-133"><span>[</span>133<span>]</span></a></sup> Truman's biography, written by his daughter Margaret, agreed with Hinde's version but did not mention the $10 initiation fee. The biography included a copy of a telegram from O.L. Chrisman stating that reporters from the [Hearst Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hearst_Corporation "Hearst Corporation") papers had questioned him about Truman's past with the Klan. He said he had seen Truman at a Klan meeting, but that "if he ever became a member of the Klan I did not know it."<sup id="cite_ref-134"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_note-134"><span>[</span>134<span>]</span></a></sup>

62.  **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States#cite_ref-time_61-0 "Jump up")** ["The Various Shady Lives of the Ku Klux Klan"](https://web.archive.org/web/20080819190502/https://time.com/time/magazine/article/0,9171,898581,00.html). _[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_(magazine) "Time (magazine)")_. April 9, 1965. Archived from [the original](https://time.com/time/magazine/article/0,9171,898581,00.html) on August 19, 2008. An itinerant Methodist preacher named William Joseph Simmons started up the Klan again in Atlanta in 1915. Simmons, an ascetic-looking man, was a fetishist on fraternal organizations. He was already a "colonel" in the Woodmen of the World, but he decided to build an organization all his own. He was an effective speaker, with an affinity for alliteration; he had preached on "Women, Weddings and Wives," "Red Heads, Dead Heads and No Heads," and the "Kinship of Kourtship and Kissing". On Thanksgiving Eve 1915, Simmons took 15 friends to the top of Stone Mountain, near Atlanta, built an altar on which he placed an American flag, a Bible and an unsheathed sword, set fire to a crude wooden cross, muttered a few incantations about a "practical fraternity among men," and declared himself Imperial Wizard of the Invisible Empire of the Knights of the Ku Klux Klan.

White men were lynched less often than Indians. Indians were regarded as living outside legal justice and therefore more likely to suffer from lynch law, much like Blacks in the South. As a hated and feared ethnic group, the Indians had first-hand experience with hangings.
